- **November 2026, Gaibandha.** The father of the Gaibandha hostage filed a case under the trafficking Act at Sundarganj police station on 5 September 2025, the case was sent to the CID for investigation, and on 12 November 2026 the CID arrested two men from Chandpur village, Singra, at Singra Bazar, Natore; the CID said it was still gathering information on where in Libya the son was held and on rescuing him <sup>[N59](#n59)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-get-help"></a>

### 2.12 Where to get help

- **In Libya:** the Bangladesh Embassy in Tripoli, the Foreign Ministry, the Ministry of Expatriates' Welfare and IOM organise the return flights <sup>[N30](#n30)</sup>. IOM gave each returnee Tk 6,000, food, medical help and temporary shelter where needed in October 2024, and travel money, food, first aid and temporary shelter as needed in 2025 and 2026 <sup>[N109 (2024)](#n109), [N111](#n111), [N113](#n113)</sup>. The embassy's reach in the east is limited: diplomatic sources said in February 2025 that Benghazi is controlled mainly by General Haftar's forces, with whom Bangladesh had not managed direct contact, so the embassy relied on an organisation called Dar al-Libya, the local Red Crescent and relatives for information <sup>[N86](#n86)</sup>. Libyan forces have freed hostages: Libya's army rescued the four Natore workers on 9 June 2024 and handed them to police custody, though the kidnappers were not caught, and the criminal investigation department of Misrata's Security Directorate freed 23 kidnapped Bangladeshis and arrested two kidnappers after a complaint at Misrata's Al-Giran police station, the embassy said on 2 April 2025, handing those freed to that station for legal process <sup>[N126 (2024)](#n126), [N127](#n127)</sup>. Libya's Anti-Illegal Migration Agency rescued 13 kidnapped Bangladeshis from a Tripoli hideout in 2026 <sup>[N128](#n128)</sup>. When 113 migrants were flown home in spring 2026, IOM paid the whole cost from the airfare to their travel back to their own villages, Md Touhid Hossain wrote; they went home with nothing <sup>[N49](#n49)</sup>.
- **In Italy:** Bangladesh Embassy, Rome, Via dell'Antartide 5-7, 00144 Rome, +39 06 808 3595, emergency +39 3511 766 537 <sup>[16](#o16)</sup>. Italy's anti-trafficking helpline 800 290 290 is free, anonymous and open 24 hours <sup>[17](#o17)</sup>.
- **At home:** a family that paid ransom can file a case with local police, as one victim did at Haziganj police station, Chandpur, in December 2025, which led the CID to arrest a man accused of coordinating the ransom demands (see [Where victims and families get help](#where-victims-and-families-get-help)) <sup>[N32](#n32), [N2](#n2)</sup>. The Deputy Commissioner of Pirojpur, asked in January 2025 about three local men held for ransom in Libya, said the administration has little it can do in such cases, but that if the family complains it can inform the Ministry of Foreign Affairs in writing <sup>[N54](#n54)</sup>. A union parishad member in Karerhat, Mirsarai, said he phoned the Libya-based broker several times to get Azmir Hossain sent home and saw the marks of beating on Azmir's head and body on a video call <sup>[N40 (2024)](#n40)</sup>. Abdur Rob's father applied in February 2025 to the Deputy Commissioner, the Superintendent of Police and the district manpower office of BMET; the office's assistant director said he would send the complaint to the embassy in Libya through the Ministry, and the Superintendent of Police said such cases are usually handled by the CID but he would help on a written complaint <sup>[N22](#n22)</sup>. For six men missing after the October 2026 sinking, the Madhabpur Upazila Nirbahi Officer said the families must apply formally to the relevant ministry, after which a search abroad can be arranged through the Foreign Ministry and the embassy <sup>[N96](#n96)</sup>. Golam Rabbani's wife applied to the Director General of the Wage Earners' Welfare Board on 8 December 2025, and went to Akkelpur police station, which said it gave her the necessary advice <sup>[N79](#n79)</sup>. A case filed in court can reach the trafficker abroad: Syed Abdus Salam's wife filed one under the trafficking law on 2 May 2024 to get him rescued, the court sent it to PBI Jashore, and its investigating officer contacted the accused, Nawab Ali, abroad; Nawab Ali put Salam on a video call with the officer and then arranged his return from Libya, and Salam came home on 13 September 2024 <sup>[N43 (2024)](#n43)</sup>. In its statement on the Gaibandha case on 13 November 2026 the CID asked everyone to verify the legality of the person, the agency, the visa and the employing firm before going abroad <sup>[N59](#n59)</sup>. The Expatriates' Welfare Minister said on 18 August 2026 that the routes of the rings were being identified for action, and that a month-long campaign was planned in several districts from Sunamganj and Habiganj to Madaripur, through the media, social media and mosques, temples, churches and other religious institutions <sup>[N129](#n129)</sup>. BRAC has started a campaign with the slogan "No going abroad by sea", working through police, mosque imams, religious leaders, schools and social media, Shariful Islam Hasan said on 18 August 2026 <sup>[N45](#n45)</sup>. IOM ran a travelling film show, "Cinema Angina", from 13 October to 17 November 2025 in 13 municipalities and about 480 villages of Madaripur, Faridpur and Shariatpur, supported by Italy's foreign ministry, to make young people aware of the risks of unsafe migration <sup>[N130](#n130)</sup>.
- **In Greece:** the State Minister for Foreign Affairs said on 3 April 2026 that quick steps were being taken to bring home Bangladeshis held in camps in Greece <sup>[N131](#n131)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What are the Bangladesh Embassy in Tripoli's working phone, WhatsApp and email for families of people held in Libya? Its website's contact page is empty.
> - Does IOM Bangladesh take requests from families directly to trace a person held in Libya, and on what number?

<a id="from-the-gulf-overland-through-iran-and-turkey-to-greece"></a>

## 3 From the Gulf overland through Iran and Turkey to Greece

<a id="how-it-is-sold-1"></a>

### 3.1 How it is sold

Faridul Alam of Chandanaish, Chattogram, who had worked in Dubai since 2003, told a columnist writing in Prothom Alo in December 2024 that he made a verbal contract of BDT 5 lakh with a Bangladeshi broker from Noakhali to be taken as far as Greece: BDT 50,000 in advance, BDT 1.5 lakh after reaching Iran and the remaining BDT 3 lakh after reaching Turkey <sup>[N27 (2024)](#n27)</sup>. By his account, after he reached Iran a relative in Dubai deposited BDT 1.5 lakh into an Iranian bank account the brokers gave, and the balance was paid into a bank account the brokers gave while the group was held in Istanbul <sup>[N27 (2024)](#n27)</sup>.

<a id="the-path-1"></a>

### 3.2 The path

1. <a id="the-path-1-item-1"></a>**Dubai to Oman by taxi.** By Faridul Alam's account, at the end of 2010 the smugglers put him and several others in a taxi in Dubai that crossed the UAE border into a coastal hill area of Oman though the passengers had no passport or visa <sup>[N27 (2024)](#n27)</sup>.
2. <a id="the-path-1-item-2"></a>**Oman to Iran by boat.** From there about thirty young men were put on a boat to Bandar Abbas in Iran, where another Bangladeshi broker received them <sup>[N27 (2024)](#n27)</sup>.
3. <a id="the-path-1-item-3"></a>**Iran to Istanbul.** He said the group was kept about seven days in Iran and taken by taxi and then bus into Turkey, that police took them off the bus on the way to Istanbul and sent them back to the border six times, and that the broker's men sent them again each time until they reached Istanbul on the seventh attempt <sup>[N27 (2024)](#n27)</sup>.
4. <a id="the-path-1-item-4"></a>**Istanbul to Greece by lorry.** He said that in Istanbul many people were kept crammed in one room and not allowed outside for more than a month, and that about a hundred people were then loaded for Greece into a closed lorry-like vehicle with room for 25 to 30 at most <sup>[N27 (2024)](#n27)</sup>.

Sujan Mia of Simtola village, Chhatak upazila, said on 18 April 2026 that he first went from Bangladesh to Oman for construction work, moved to Dubai after a problem with his iqama (residence permit) and then worked in Qatar, and later returned to Dubai to head for Europe, going through Oman to Iran, Turkey, Greece, North Macedonia and Serbia, working illegally to survive <sup>[N48](#n48)</sup>. Both men later went on through the Balkans (see [The Balkans and Eastern Europe](#the-balkans-and-eastern-europe)) <sup>[N27 (2024)](#n27), [N48](#n48)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens"></a>

### 3.3 What happens

- **Arrival in Greece.** By Faridul Alam's account, Greek soldiers or border guards caught the group where the lorry dropped them and put them in jail, UN refugee agency staff then moved them to a refugee camp, and after about ten days they were given a document called a "stay paper" and took a bus to Athens with the EUR 60 per person the smugglers had handed them in Istanbul <sup>[N27 (2024)](#n27)</sup>.
- **A false nationality.** He said he presented himself as Rohingya in all his asylum applications in Greece because the smuggling ring's brokers advised that asylum might be easier that way; the court did not accept his application and stamped his stay paper, which let him stay one month, and he never got asylum in Greece despite four or five interviews <sup>[N27 (2024)](#n27)</sup>.
- **Nothing to send home.** He said he lived about four years in Athens hawking and doing manual work, with police at times detaining him and taking his goods, and that what he earned fed him and left nothing to send to his family in Bangladesh <sup>[N27 (2024)](#n27)</sup>.
- **Refused again further on.** His asylum applications were rejected in Germany, where he lived on EUR 350 a month from a refugee camp from 2015 to 2018, and again in Paris, where the process took from 2018 to 2021; in December 2024 he was cooking for a mess of Bangladeshis in La Courneuve, Paris <sup>[N27 (2024)](#n27)</sup>.
- **No passport to come home on.** He said the Bangladesh embassy in Paris took his application and fingerprints for a passport and, after a long wait, told him it could not issue a new passport unless he submitted his old Bangladeshi one, which he had thrown away when he set out from Dubai because, he said, an asylum application cannot be made while holding it <sup>[N27 (2024)](#n27)</sup>.
- **Deported from Turkey after 25 years.** Mohammad Farid of Ukhiya, Cox's Bazar, crossed the border illegally at 10 with his father and went through India to Pakistan, where he lost both legs to a train; according to BRAC's Migration Programme, a trafficking ring then lured him with a better life, a job and Europe and took him to Turkey, where he sold water on the seashore as an irregular migrant until Turkish law enforcement detained him and sent him back <sup>[N132](#n132)</sup>. He landed at Dhaka airport on 18 August 2026 able to give only his own and his parents' names and "Ukhiya, Cox's Bazar" (see [Where victims and families get help](#where-victims-and-families-get-help)) <sup>[N132](#n132)</sup>.

<a id="waiting-in-iran"></a>

### 3.4 Waiting in Iran

Foreign Ministry officials said in June 2025 that more than 2,000 Bangladeshis were in Iran, that about 800 had been working there illegally in various sectors for more than ten years, and that 300 to 500 Bangladeshis are always in Iran, used as a transit country, waiting to be trafficked onward <sup>[N133](#n133)</sup>. A Prothom Alo editorial of 19 June 2025 said those doing small jobs such as fishing in the sea areas were the most exposed, many had no valid papers, and some Bangladeshis were in Iranian detention centres <sup>[N134](#n134)</sup>.

**Getting out in a crisis:** when the government brought Bangladeshis home from Iran through Azerbaijan during the war in March 2026, not all had passports, and the Bangladesh Embassy in Tehran gave those without one a travel pass; IOM helped get people from Tehran and elsewhere in Iran to Baku airport, and a Biman flight left Baku with 186 on 20 March <sup>[N135](#n135), [N136](#n136)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - How long do people waiting in Iran to be moved on usually stay there, and where (Tehran, Bandar Abbas, the Turkish border)?

<a id="through-west-africa-and-the-sahara-to-morocco"></a>

## 4 Through West Africa and the Sahara to Morocco

A former Bangladeshi army officer who served as a UN military observer in Western Sahara wrote in Prothom Alo in June 2024 that on 5 April 2005 he learned that 46 Bangladeshis were held by the Sahrawi People's Liberation Army (the Western Sahara independence movement's army) at its Tifariti camp <sup>[N137 (2024)](#n137)</sup>. By his account:

- **The route:** each had spent Tk 10 to 12 lakh and travelled in small groups from Dhaka through the UAE, Mali and Mauritania to Western Sahara, some having left home four months and some twelve months earlier, heading for Europe <sup>[N137 (2024)](#n137)</sup>.
- **Pushed back:** when they crossed into Morocco, the Moroccan army detained them and pushed them back to the Sahrawi side <sup>[N137 (2024)](#n137)</sup>.
- **In the camp:** they worked all day and got one meal of bread and lentils <sup>[N137 (2024)](#n137)</sup>.
- **Home:** he collected voluntary return assistance forms and filled them in for all 46; it took about ten months to free them and bring them home, the group being due to fly from Mauritania to Dhaka on 15 or 16 January 2006 <sup>[N137 (2024)](#n137)</sup>.

<a id="the-balkans-and-eastern-europe"></a>

## 5 The Balkans and Eastern Europe

<a id="how-it-is-sold-2"></a>

### 5.1 How it is sold

The product is a "work permit" or "work-permit visa" for an Eastern European country, sold by individuals and some agencies for lakhs of taka <sup>[N11 (2021)](#n11)</sup>. A Sunamganj father sold land and paid a dalal Tk 4.5 lakh, about two years before April 2026, to send his son to Serbia; it never happened and the money could not be recovered <sup>[N67](#n67)</sup>. Victims report that the papers the agency hands over are declared fake once the file reaches the embassy, and that one permit is resold to several people with the name changed <sup>[N11 (2021)](#n11)</sup>. In April 2026 thirteen countries' missions, including Italy, Hungary and Germany, jointly warned Bangladeshis against false documents, unlicensed agents and payments to anyone claiming special access; none of them works with any agent <sup>[N138](#n138)</sup>. A Romanian job sold for EUR 11,000 (about Tk 15.4 lakh) turned out to be undeclared building work, 12 hours a day, 7 days a week <sup>[N139](#n139), [7](#o7)</sup>. Thousands of Bangladeshis have paid up to EUR 20,000 for Italian work permits under Italy's quota scheme (Decreto Flussi) that never came <sup>[N140](#n140)</sup>.

<a id="the-path-2"></a>

### 5.2 The path

- **Legal entry, then onward.** Workers go legally to a cheaper EU country such as Romania, lose the job or the papers, and move on to Italy <sup>[N140](#n140)</sup>. One man paid EUR 300 more for the Romanian nulla osta (permission) he needed to change jobs, then flew to Italy to join a cousin, Romania now being in the Schengen area (the EU zone without border checks) <sup>[N139](#n139)</sup>.
- **Overland from Greece.** Faridul Alam, who had reached Greece from Dubai through Iran and Turkey (see [From the Gulf overland through Iran and Turkey to Greece](#from-the-gulf-overland-through-iran-and-turkey-to-greece)), said that at the end of 2014 he left Athens by bus with two or three other Bangladeshis for Macedonia, then Serbia, then Hungary, where the bus of Afghan, Pakistani and Bangladeshi refugees was taken to a refugee camp; he then left for Austria and Germany, and in the Czech Republic police held him eight to ten days and took the money and papers in his bag and pockets and did not return them <sup>[N27 (2024)](#n27)</sup>. Sujan Mia, who had also come from the Gulf, said that, finding no chance of legal work in the countries he passed through, he went back to Greece and from there travelled through Bulgaria, Albania and Bosnia and across Italy to France, where he worked about four years and eight months and tried to regularise; though he paid tax and completed the process, he was caught before he got legal papers and came back to Bangladesh <sup>[N48](#n48)</sup>.
- **Overland through Bosnia.** In September 2026 Bosnian border police stopped 96 people, 36 of them Bangladeshis, crossing illegally at Bistrica near Gradiska, and seized two boats <sup>[N141](#n141)</sup>; the police said the groups were preparing to use the boats to cross the Sava river on the border, and that the first group of 63 held 23 Bangladeshis and 40 Pakistanis <sup>[N142](#n142)</sup>.
- **Refused, then the boat.** A Cumilla man spent three years trying Serbia, Romania, Slovenia and Belarus legally, was refused every time, then went irregularly <sup>[N16](#n16)</sup>.
- **Through Central Asia.** Brokers have taken men sold "Poland" or "Europe" to Kyrgyzstan instead (see [Kyrgyzstan and Central Asia as a stepping stone](#kyrgyzstan-and-central-asia-as-a-stepping-stone)) <sup>[N143 (2023)](#n143), [N144](#n144)</sup>.
- **Through Russia and Belarus into Poland.** Liton Chowdhury (35) of Juri, Moulvibazar, who had lived in Dubai, arranged from there to be taken to Germany by a broker living in Russia known as Max, whose home is in Noakhali, for Tk 8.25 lakh, according to his brother-in-law Shahin Ahmad <sup>[N145 (2024)](#n145)</sup>. The family paid the money to Max's relative Nazrul Islam, a car dealer in Beanibazar, Sylhet, under a written agreement that all of it would be returned if Liton was not sent to Germany <sup>[N145 (2024)](#n145)</sup>. By Shahin's account, in September 2024 Liton went through Russia to Belarus, was caught by police with several others on reaching Poland and sent back across the border, and Belarus police would not let them back in, so they lived in the forest between the two countries <sup>[N145 (2024)](#n145)</sup>. His last WhatsApp voice message, on 7 October, said he had no food or water and a broken leg; Max only kept giving assurances, and Liton's number then went dead <sup>[N145 (2024)](#n145)</sup>. A local news video from the border claimed Polish police had beaten 15 migrants to death, and a Bangladeshi doctor in Belarus who searched refugee camps and hospitals found Liton's body in a hospital morgue in November 2024 <sup>[N145 (2024)](#n145)</sup>. Max and Nazrul gave no help bringing the body home; Kulaura police said they would investigate on a written complaint <sup>[N145 (2024)](#n145)</sup>.
- **Fake visa on the plane.** In December 2022 a Bangladeshi flying from Abu Dhabi to Krakow, Poland, on a fully forged Polish work visa bought for about USD 2,000 was refused entry and charged <sup>[18 (2022)](#o18)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-1"></a>

### 5.3 What happens

- **Serbia:** more than 40 Bangladeshis recruited by Bangladeshi agencies reached the Linglong tyre factory in June 2025; rights groups reported possible trafficking <sup>[N146](#n146)</sup>. Many had borrowed up to USD 10,000 and had passports taken on arrival; workers said they were paid below Serbia's minimum wage of about USD 660 a month, and their contracts named a Serbian middleman company, not the factory <sup>[N146](#n146)</sup>.
- **Eastern Europe generally:** workers find the pay lower than promised, and lower still after tax <sup>[N11 (2021)](#n11)</sup>. Employers in Poland, Hungary, Bulgaria, Slovenia, Croatia, Kosovo, Albania and Montenegro are increasingly reluctant to hire Bangladeshis because many leave for Western Europe <sup>[N104](#n104)</sup>.
- **Being sent back:** about 5,000 Bangladeshis have been returned from Europe under a 2017 return agreement, most from Cyprus, Italy, Romania and France <sup>[N147](#n147)</sup>. The agreement, Standard Operating Procedures (SOP) signed with the European Union in 2017 to send back about 90,000 irregular migrants, provides that a Bangladeshi in Europe without valid status who is not granted asylum after the full legal process is sent to Bangladesh, researchers of North South University's Centre for Migration Studies wrote in May 2024 <sup>[N103 (2024)](#n103)</sup>.
- **Visa refusals:** in 2025 Schengen consulates in Bangladesh refused 54.5% of applications, and the Italian ambassador links high refusals to fake documents <sup>[19](#o19), [20](#o20)</sup>. A forged document leads to refusal and a flag in the Schengen system that affects all future EU travel <sup>[21](#o21)</sup>.

<a id="the-numbers-1"></a>

### 5.4 The numbers

- **The Balkan route:** Frontex recorded more than 4,900 irregular border crossings on the Balkan route in January to July 2026, 26% fewer than a year earlier <sup>[N142](#n142)</sup>.
- **Legal flows:** 18,220 Bangladeshis migrated legally to Europe from January to 25 June 2026 <sup>[N104](#n104)</sup>.
- **The risky "new" countries:** from January 2025 to 30 October 2026, 44,122 Bangladeshis got BMET clearance for 12 countries the Ministry now restricts, 27,647 of them for Cambodia, Russia, Laos and Belarus <sup>[N148](#n148)</sup>. Cambodia, Russia, Laos and Belarus are on the lowest tier (Tier 3) of the US State Department's 2026 Trafficking in Persons report <sup>[N149](#n149)</sup>.
- **Visa processing:** applications for several European countries are handled by embassies outside Bangladesh, mainly in India <sup>[N104](#n104)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-1"></a>

### 5.5 The law

- **Bangladesh:** on 4 November 2026 the Ministry of Expatriates' Welfare told BMET to give no emigration clearance for 12 countries without the Ministry's recruitment permission or approval <sup>[N150](#n150)</sup>. Every published list names Serbia, Moldova, Belarus, Japan, Iraq, Laos, Vietnam, Ukraine and Libya; most also name Russia and Cambodia; the rest differ between reports (Congo, Kosovo, Croatia or Bosnia) <sup>[N150](#n150), [N151](#n151), [N152](#n152), [N149](#n149)</sup>. As Prothom Alo reported it on 5 November 2026, the letter to BMET's Director General names Serbia, Moldova, Belarus, Congo, Russia, Japan, Iraq, Laos, Vietnam, Cambodia, Ukraine and Libya, and BAIRA (the Bangladesh Association of International Recruiting Agencies) sent a copy of the instruction to all its member agencies the same day <sup>[N153](#n153)</sup>. Getting or selling a demand letter, visa or work permit by illegal means is a crime (see [The law in Bangladesh](#the-law-in-bangladesh)) <sup>[2](#o2)</sup>.
- **EU:** forging a visa or using one is punished in Italy by 1 to 6 years in prison <sup>[22](#o22)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-get-help-1"></a>

### 5.6 Where to get help

Report a fake permit offer to BMET or the CID (see [Where victims and families get help](#where-victims-and-families-get-help)) <sup>[4](#o4), [N29](#n29)</sup>. People in the sector told Prothom Alo in November 2026 that workers interested in jobs in the 12 restricted countries should hold back from any payment or final decision until the appointment letter, the Ministry's approval and the emigration clearance have all been confirmed <sup>[N153](#n153)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What is the street price in 2026 of a Serbia, Croatia or Romania "work permit" sold in Dhaka or Sylhet, and how is it paid?
> - Is anyone still being sent through Russia and Belarus to cross into Poland or Lithuania in 2025 and 2026, and what does that leg cost?
> - What is the exact list of 12 countries in the Ministry's 4 November 2026 letter?

<a id="the-united-states-through-south-america-and-mexico"></a>

## 6 The United States through South America and Mexico

<a id="how-it-is-sold-3"></a>

### 6.1 How it is sold

Dr Delwar Arif, an associate professor at Canisius University in New York, wrote in a Prothom Alo column on 27 March 2026 that a ring took crores of taka from thousands of people in the name of sending them to the United States, through well-furnished offices in upmarket areas of Dhaka and eye-catching advertisements <sup>[N154](#n154)</sup>. According to the column, the advertisements say "Do you want to settle in America?" and "Get a green card in America by investing only Tk 50 to 60 lakh"; the writer says they take Tk 50 to 60 lakh a head only to bring a person across the southern or northern border of the United States, and that the dalals and traffickers do not care what happens after arrival or whether the person is caught <sup>[N154](#n154)</sup>.

<a id="the-path-3"></a>

### 6.2 The path

- **Through Suriname.** According to a US Embassy case statement of February 2024, one of the accused, Mofizur Rahman, traffics people from Bangladesh to Dubai and, with other dalals, to the United States, and sent his own brother from Bangladesh first through India to Suriname in South America, from where the brother went to the United States across the Mexico border <sup>[N28 (2024)](#n28)</sup>.
- **Through Paraguay.** According to the same statement, another accused, Ashraful Alam Bhuiyan, said in his visa interview that he helps traffic people to the United States through Paraguay in South America <sup>[N28 (2024)](#n28)</sup>.
- **Eleven countries in nine months.** Hossain, a rice farmer from Feni whose land had flooded repeatedly and who said ruling-party members were threatening him over his opposition politics, sold his land and borrowed about USD 33,000 from relatives and friends, raising USD 41,000 in all for travel and the dalal's costs, according to an investigation by Columbia Journalism Investigations (CJI) and Documented republished by Prothom Alo in October 2025 <sup>[N155](#n155)</sup>. He left Bangladesh in February 2023 and crossed 11 countries by air and land over nine months before giving himself up to border officers in the US state of Arizona, who held him for three days in a room with 15 people and then released him <sup>[N155](#n155)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-2"></a>

### 6.3 What happens

- **Asylum.** A legal firm in Manhattan filed Hossain's asylum application in April 2024 on the ground that he supports a Bangladeshi opposition party; the effect of climate-driven floods on his income and livelihood is not an accepted ground for asylum, and the investigation says the United States has no specific provision protecting people who flee climate disasters <sup>[N155](#n155)</sup>.
- **Work while waiting.** While waiting for his hearing in the immigration court, Hossain got a US work permit after seven months; he worked first as a kitchen helper in a local restaurant, then delivering food by bike in Brooklyn, and in 2025 worked full time, six days a week, at a Bengali sweet shop and cafe in Kensington, Brooklyn, sharing a nearby basement apartment with two roommates <sup>[N155](#n155)</sup>. The investigation says Bangladeshi migrants arriving in New York first settle around the Bangladeshi grocery shops and restaurants of Kensington, and that an estimated 1 lakh Bangladeshis live in New York City <sup>[N155](#n155)</sup>.
- **Detention.** Officers of US Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) detained two Bangladeshis from Noakhali who were working in Buffalo, New York, and Hossain keeps copies of his asylum application and court hearing notices to hand and his lawyer's number on speed dial in case ICE comes to his home <sup>[N155](#n155)</sup>.
- **Family left behind.** Hossain had hoped that once granted asylum he could apply to bring his wife and daughter; he said that had he known how hard it now is, he would not have come to the United States <sup>[N155](#n155)</sup>.
- **Fake lawyers in the US.** Dr Delwar Arif wrote that, according to reports aired by ABC's Channel 7, MSN and Bangla channels in the United States, a Bangladeshi in New York who posed as an immigration lawyer without being one, and his firm, took millions of dollars from Bangladeshi and other legal and illegal immigration-seekers in the United States, USD 60 thousand from one person alone <sup>[N154](#n154)</sup>. He writes that in nearly two decades in the United States he has heard of many cases of people being cheated through Bangladeshi immigration lawyers, that many highly educated Bangladeshis are among the victims, and that US embassies could protect would-be migrants through public awareness campaigns and joint work with local law enforcement <sup>[N154](#n154)</sup>.

<a id="the-numbers-2"></a>

### 6.4 The numbers

- **Growth:** according to CJI and Documented's analysis of US Customs and Border Protection records, border crossings from Bangladesh rose 150% from 2019 to 2024 <sup>[N155](#n155)</sup>.
- **Home districts:** in the same analysis Noakhali is the top district of origin, with more than 30% of the Bangladeshis detained at the border, and Feni is among the top 10 Bangladeshi towns from which people reached the US-Mexico border <sup>[N155](#n155)</sup>.
- **2024 detentions:** among people detained in 2024 whose records the investigation examined, about 100 were born in Bangladesh, against more than 520 born in Guatemala and about 350 in Senegal <sup>[N155](#n155)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-2"></a>

### 6.5 The law

- **Bangladesh:** a US Embassy official filed a case at Gulshan police station, Dhaka, in February 2024 against five Bangladeshis on charges of involvement in human trafficking; the case says two of them, Mofizur Rahman (54) and Ashraful Alam Bhuiyan (33), went for US visa interviews on 11 February and admitted during the interviews that they are involved in trafficking people from Bangladesh to various countries and work mainly as dalals <sup>[N28 (2024)](#n28)</sup>. The case names two associates, Mohammad Zaman and Bhasani, and says a man named Mohammad Nur Alam heads the ring; the police Counter Terrorism and Transnational Crime (CTTC) unit was given the investigation, and its deputy commissioner said in February 2024 that the process of formally taking over the case file from Gulshan police station had begun <sup>[N28 (2024)](#n28)</sup>.

<a id="the-visitor-visa-bond"></a>

### 6.6 The visitor visa bond

- **The rule.** From 21 January 2026, Bangladeshi citizens approved for a US business or tourist visa (B1/B2) must post a bond of up to USD 15,000 (about Tk 18 lakh 35 thousand at Tk 122.31 a dollar), the US Embassy in Dhaka said in a notice on its verified Facebook page on 19 January 2026; the condition does not apply to holders of valid B1/B2 visas issued before that date <sup>[N156](#n156)</sup>. The US State Department's travel website said on 6 January 2026 that citizens of 38 countries including Bangladesh must post the bond, and the embassy's notice said the money is returned if the visa's conditions are properly followed <sup>[N156](#n156)</sup>.
- **How it is paid.** An applicant found eligible after the visa interview is given payment instructions by the consular officer with a direct link to pay.gov, and must pay the bond within 30 days, the embassy said on 26 January 2026 <sup>[N157](#n157)</sup>.
- **When it is lost.** The visa issued under the bond pilot programme is single-entry and valid for at most three months, the holder must enter the United States through certain designated ports of entry, and the bond is cancelled automatically and the money returned after the holder comes home if all the visa's conditions are met, among them not working in the United States and returning within the set time, the embassy said <sup>[N157](#n157)</sup>.

<a id="visit-and-tourist-visas-sold-as-jobs"></a>

## 7 Visit and tourist visas sold as jobs

<a id="how-it-is-sold-4"></a>

### 7.1 How it is sold

The Ministry of Expatriates' Welfare warned in February 2025 that brokers lure people with pay and jobs and send them on tourist or visit visas to the Middle East, Russia, Greece, Libya, Lebanon, Egypt, Cambodia, Malaysia and elsewhere <sup>[23](#o23)</sup>. Brokers tell people they have a work visa: to Saudi Arabia, allegedly, a three-month tourist visa sold with a promised 15-month iqama (residence permit); to Cambodia, a one or two-month tourist visa sold as a two-year work visa <sup>[N17 (2024)](#n17), [N15](#n15)</sup>. Women are sent to Dubai on tourist or visit visas for promised jobs in dance bars, beauty parlours, restaurants or homes <sup>[N2](#n2)</sup>. Some victims went with BMET clearance and still landed on a visit visa <sup>[N158](#n158)</sup>. Ali Akbar Khan, acting additional inspector general of police at the CID (the Criminal Investigation Department of the police), said at a Prothom Alo roundtable in July 2026 that many people go abroad on tourist visas and are then cheated, and that some go from being victims to becoming traffickers themselves <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>. For Canada, a Prothom Alo report from Canada in April 2024 said each person who came on a visit visa had spent Tk 5 to 15 lakh on it, because the visa is document-based and dishonest rings made the financial and other documents for them <sup>[N160 (2024)](#n160)</sup>. The Maldives gives Bangladeshi tourists a visa at the airport, and dalals use this to cheat workers <sup>[N161 (2024)](#n161)</sup>.

<a id="the-path-country-by-country"></a>

### 7.2 The path, country by country

- **UAE (Dubai):**
  - The UAE market closed to Bangladeshis in 2012 after complaints about workers; about 25,000 a year still went in 2014 and 2015, 1,000 in 2020, and sending resumed in 2021 <sup>[N162 (2024)](#n162)</sup>.
  - After the Covid pandemic the UAE began taking workers on travel visas, with the employer applying after arrival to change it to an employment visa; agency owners said in 2024 that a ring used this to cheat workers, many of whom found no work and lived on the streets and in parks <sup>[N162 (2024)](#n162)</sup>. Shakirul Islam, chair of OKUP (Ovibashi Karmi Unnayan Program, a migrant workers' organisation), said workers who went on travel visas had to pay brokers again to get an employment visa <sup>[N162 (2024)](#n162)</sup>. Tipu Sultan, joint secretary general of BAIRA (the Bangladesh Association of International Recruiting Agencies), said many found no work, which damaged Bangladesh's image and reduced interest in its workers <sup>[N162 (2024)](#n162)</sup>.
  - BAIRA's secretary general said in June 2024 that the UAE does not give visas for hiring in the unskilled sector and wants workers in professional categories, where Bangladeshi workers lack certificates, although the visa type can be changed after arriving <sup>[N162 (2024)](#n162)</sup>. An employer found connected with a worker who has become illegal is fined AED 50,000 (UAE dirhams), one reason UAE employers became unwilling to take Bangladeshis <sup>[N162 (2024)](#n162)</sup>.
  - The UAE restricted both work and visit visas for Bangladeshis in July 2024, and turning a visit visa into a work visa has been closed to Bangladeshis for years <sup>[N163](#n163), [N164](#n164)</sup>. Dubai is the usual transit point to Libya and has also been used for Kyrgyzstan and Russia <sup>[N16](#n16), [N165 (2024)](#n165), [N166](#n166)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Can a Bangladeshi on a UAE visit visa have it changed to an employment visa inside the UAE in 2026? Agency leaders said in June 2024 that it could be, a January 2026 report says it has been closed for several years, and the Bangladesh Embassy said in September 2025 that no UAE visa ban had been announced.

- **Saudi Arabia:** a visit visa can't be turned into an iqama; offers to convert one for a fee are fraud <sup>[N167 (2022)](#n167)</sup>. Saudi Arabia stopped new business, tourist and family visit visas for Bangladeshis from April to June 2025 after people used them to do Hajj <sup>[N168](#n168), [N169](#n169)</sup>. An immigration official said traffickers sometimes use a valid visa for one country, such as a tourist or Umrah (pilgrimage) visa, before sending people elsewhere <sup>[N2](#n2)</sup>.
- **Oman:** since 31 October 2023 a visit visa can't be converted to a work visa in Oman; the person must leave and come back on a work visa <sup>[N170 (2023)](#n170)</sup>.
- **Kuwait:** every visit visa holder is barred from working and must leave at the end of the visit <sup>[24](#o24)</sup>. Only some visit visas can be converted, for KD 150 (Kuwaiti dinars) <sup>[25](#o25)</sup>.
- **Qatar:** a visit visa can't be converted inside Qatar to a work visa <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>. In 2014, 23 Bangladeshis brought on tourist visas with job promises had no work for three months, overstayed and were arrested <sup>[N171 (2014)](#n171)</sup>.
- **Malaysia:** in October 2026 the CID arrested a Paltan travel agency owner accused of taking two people to Malaysia on visit visas with a promise of work permits, holding them and demanding Tk 5 lakh from their families <sup>[N172](#n172)</sup>. In September 2026 Malaysian immigration detained 147 Bangladeshis on Sepang building sites where employers had misused social visit passes <sup>[N173](#n173)</sup>. A woman taken to Malaysia on a visit visa on 15 November 2025 for a promised restaurant job said her passport, ticket and papers were taken on arrival and she was told she would get no work unless she paid the balance, though she had agreed to pay the last Tk 5,000 after starting work (see "A visit or tourist visa sold as a job" in the Dalal scams doc) <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>.
- **Maldives:** workers land through Kuala Lumpur, Chennai or Malé with a forged 12-month work visa that is really a three-month tourist visa <sup>[N174 (2023)](#n174)</sup>. Some workers went in on the airport tourist visa without the embassy's attestation, and in 2024 they were being identified <sup>[N161 (2024)](#n161)</sup>. The High Commission says a tourist can never get a work visa there, and trying to work on one can mean jail and a fine <sup>[27](#o27)</sup>.
- **Nepal:** police officers, speaking anonymously in October 2026, said the government had been warned that Nepal is being used as a route for trafficking people from Bangladesh, and that because Nepal gives visas on arrival, documents are checked with special attention at the airport and a passenger whose papers look suspicious is stopped <sup>[N175](#n175)</sup>. Three adult women with travel papers and tickets for a 10:05 am flight to Nepal on 8 August 2026 allege that the immigration police officer-in-charge refused to let them travel and gave no written notice or legal reason; the officers said they could not show an invitation to the seminar they named, and on 29 September 2026 the High Court issued a rule asking why stopping them should not be declared unlawful <sup>[N175](#n175)</sup>.
- **Canada:** in 2024 Canada announced that people who found an LMIA-approved job (one for which no one already allowed to work in Canada could be found; LMIA is the Labour Market Impact Assessment) could come and work temporarily, and many Bangladeshis who came on visit visas without understanding this found no such job and claimed political asylum <sup>[N160 (2024)](#n160)</sup>.

<a id="the-numbers-3"></a>

### 7.3 The numbers

- **UAE:** BMET data show about 8,500 workers a month went to the UAE in 2022, 8,200 a month in 2023 and 6,500 a month in the first four months of 2024 <sup>[N162 (2024)](#n162)</sup>.
- **Maldives:** the market closed in 2019, when 4,411 workers went; after the pandemic began the Maldives caught workers without valid papers and sent them home, 16,000 between March and December 2021, then regularised about 40,000 irregular Bangladeshis before the market reopened in December 2023 <sup>[N161 (2024)](#n161)</sup>. In May 2024 there were about 100,000 legal Bangladeshi workers there and possibly at least 30,000 more irregular ones <sup>[N161 (2024)](#n161)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-3"></a>

### 7.4 What happens

- **No legal work:** the Ministry of Expatriates' Welfare says a tourist or visit visa gives no legal scope to work, so people sent on one suffer financial and mental harm and are put at risk, and deaths are occurring on illegal routes <sup>[N176](#n176)</sup>.
- **Debt bondage and sex work:** women taken to Dubai have passports and phones taken, are confined, and are given invented debts of USD 10,000 to 15,000 that some are forced to work off in sex work <sup>[N2](#n2)</sup>.
- **Ransom:** the Malaysia visit-visa case above ended in a ransom demand <sup>[N172](#n172)</sup>.
- **Overstay and deportation:** visit visas expire, the person becomes illegal and is arrested (Qatar 2014, Kuwait, Maldives) <sup>[N171 (2014)](#n171), [24](#o24), [27](#o27)</sup>.
- **Airport collusion:** allegations reported in November 2026 say brokers pay some airport staff Tk 30,000 to 50,000 per passenger, called a "body contract", to pass immigration with incomplete papers <sup>[N2](#n2)</sup>.
- **Canada, as reported in April 2024** (see also Asylum claims built on a false story) <sup>[N160 (2024)](#n160)</sup>:
  - Travellers were being turned back at airports before entering Canada, documents were checked again on landing, and those with fake invitation letters and fake documents were sent home at once <sup>[N160 (2024)](#n160)</sup>.
  - Visit-visa applicants had shown a good job or business, large bank balances and ties at home, then told a different story to claim asylum, so many were expected to lose their cases and be removed <sup>[N160 (2024)](#n160)</sup>.
  - An asylum claimant must prove his life is under threat at home and must hire an immigration lawyer, who charges at least CAD 7,000 to 10,000; a claimant who loses is removed <sup>[N160 (2024)](#n160)</sup>.
  - Claimants got roughly 700 dollars a month from the government until they found work and had to hand over their passports, so could not go home; thousands could find no work for lack of English, relevant experience and education <sup>[N160 (2024)](#n160)</sup>.
  - Many were living on food banks, which had cut what they give as crowds grew, and were bringing large sums from home through hundi to fight their cases <sup>[N160 (2024)](#n160)</sup>.
  - A ring inside Canada was sending people on to the United States across unguarded border points near Montreal, and various sources said many were held in US jails <sup>[N160 (2024)](#n160)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-3"></a>

### 7.5 The law

- **Bangladesh:** the Ministry's rule is never go to work on a tourist or visit visa on a broker's promise, and never go without BMET clearance <sup>[23](#o23)</sup>. The same notice says to confirm with BMET that the job and the recruiting agency are genuine, and not to go abroad for work through anyone other than a valid recruiting agency <sup>[N176](#n176)</sup>. Taking money on a false promise of a job abroad is punished by up to 5 years in prison and a fine of at least Tk 1 lakh <sup>[2](#o2)</sup>.
- **Destinations:** Kuwait bars every visit visa holder from working, and in the Maldives trying to work on a tourist visa can mean jail and a fine (see The path, country by country) <sup>[24](#o24), [27](#o27)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-get-help-2"></a>

### 7.6 Where to get help

The Probash-Bondhu call centre answers workers and families 24 hours (see [Where victims and families get help](#where-victims-and-families-get-help)) <sup>[28](#o28)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What does a Dubai or Saudi tourist visa "with job" sell for in Dhaka in 2026?

<a id="free-visas-and-visa-trading-in-the-gulf"></a>

## 8 Free visas and visa trading in the Gulf

<a id="how-it-is-sold-5"></a>

### 8.1 How it is sold

A "free visa" (also called "azad visa", meaning a free or independent visa) is a visa the worker buys that is not tied to a real job <sup>[N12](#n12)</sup>. Under kafala, the Gulf sponsorship system, the visa is issued for one employer, and the sponsor lets the worker work elsewhere in return for a fee <sup>[N177 (2016)](#n177)</sup>. Brokers and some agencies tell workers they will easily find work and earn a lot once there, and charge Tk 4 to 7 lakh for Qatar <sup>[N6](#n6), [N178](#n178)</sup>. Dr Selim Reza of North South University's Centre for Migration Studies wrote in May 2026 that in practice a "free visa" means sending a worker without any specific job, a form of visa trading from which brokers, recruiting agencies and some dishonest businessmen profit <sup>[N178](#n178)</sup>. OKUP (Ovibashi Karmi Unnayan Program, a migrant rights group) found free-visa migrants to the Gulf paid about Tk 5 lakh, against the government-set average of Tk 1.16 lakh <sup>[N179](#n179)</sup>. Of those who knowingly bought one, 54% went through illegal broker networks, 31% through distant relatives, 8% through family and 7% directly through recruiting agencies <sup>[N179](#n179)</sup>. In Kuwait an organised ring sells visas for lakhs of taka under the "free visa" name <sup>[N180](#n180)</sup>.

Qatar's own request is for skilled workers: at the seventh Bangladesh-Qatar joint committee meeting on 18 May 2026 Qatar showed interest in technical workers, especially electricians, plumbers, welders and AC technicians, and its labour minister said Qatar wants skilled workers directly from five specific TTCs (Technical Training Centres) in Bangladesh <sup>[N178](#n178)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Which five TTCs did Qatar name, and how does a trainee there get onto that direct channel?

<a id="what-happens-4"></a>

### 8.2 What happens

- **No job:** 43% of free-visa migrants found no work on arrival <sup>[N181](#n181)</sup>. The visa is tied to a sponsor with no job, and most end up in low-paid work or broken contracts <sup>[N180](#n180)</sup>. Workers who spend Tk 4 to 7 lakh on a Qatar "free visa" find a company's name on the papers but no work behind it, Selim Reza wrote in May 2026 <sup>[N178](#n178)</sup>. A Bangladeshi worker in Riyadh wrote in August 2025 that dalal rings bring workers through various agencies on false promises but cannot give them work, so many hunt for work themselves; he had seen cheated workers sleeping on the street and one preparing to kill himself under the weight of debt <sup>[N182](#n182)</sup>.
- **Paying again:** 21% had to pay about Tk 1.5 lakh more after arrival to get an iqama, and 14% could not pay, got no permit and stayed illegally <sup>[N179](#n179)</sup>. In Qatar, sponsors sometimes demand regular payments to keep sponsoring <sup>[N183](#n183)</sup>.
- **Absconding cases:** brokers in Kuwait file false "absconding" (runaway) reports to free up visa quotas for resale, and some sponsors in Oman report free-visa workers as absconding so they are not punished if the worker is caught elsewhere <sup>[N184](#n184), [N177 (2016)](#n177)</sup>.
- **No rights:** in Qatar a free-visa worker has no legal help if unpaid <sup>[29 (2021)](#o29)</sup>. Free-visa workers there hunt for illegal day labour and many face harassment, arrest or deportation <sup>[N6](#n6), [N178](#n178)</sup>.
- **Qatar in 2026:** Selim Reza wrote in May 2026 that countless Bangladeshi workers wait for temporary work in and around Doha, some get no work for weeks, and many go unpaid for months <sup>[N178](#n178)</sup>. He wrote that thousands are without work or pay, a large share jobless after being cheated through visa trading and broker rings, some undocumented and many without food or medical care <sup>[N178](#n178)</sup>. After the 2022 football World Cup, he wrote, employment fell in much of Qatar's construction sector as companies wound up projects and laid off workers, so there are fewer openings than before <sup>[N178](#n178)</sup>.
- **Saudi Arabia:** Amnesty International says migrant workers there have been cheated by recruitment brokers, forced to work extra hours and had wages stolen, in some cases probably amounting to trafficking for labour exploitation; IOM (the International Organization for Migration) lists irregular migration, hidden work such as domestic or informal work, undue employer control, recruitment corruption and dependence on third-party brokers among the risks migrants face there <sup>[N185](#n185)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-4"></a>

### 8.3 The law

- **Bangladesh:** BMET says there is no such thing as a free visa <sup>[30](#o30)</sup>. Buying or selling a visa or work permit inside Bangladesh, or getting one illegally, is punished by 2 to 7 years' rigorous imprisonment <sup>[2](#o2)</sup>. These offences are non-bailable <sup>[2](#o2)</sup>. Selim Reza wrote in May 2026 that it must be ensured no worker can go abroad without a contract for a specific job, and that sending workers on a "free visa" or uncertain employment must stop <sup>[N178](#n178)</sup>.
- **Qatar:** visa trading, and working on a free visa, carry up to 3 years in prison and a fine of up to QR 50,000 (Qatari riyals) <sup>[31 (2015)](#o31), [29 (2021)](#o29)</sup>.
- **Oman:** an Omani caught granting free visas is fined 1,000 rials, but the practice is widespread <sup>[N186 (2021)](#n186)</sup>.
- **Kuwait:** the Bangladesh ambassador says there is no free visa in Kuwait law <sup>[N187](#n187)</sup>.
- **Maldives:** the High Commission says there is no free visa; working for another company brings arrest and deportation <sup>[1 (2024)](#o1), [N188](#n188)</sup>. Its notice of 22 May 2024 said there is no scope to work outside the approved employer and that a number of unskilled workers had been sent home for breaking this law <sup>[N161 (2024)](#n161)</sup>.

<a id="the-numbers-4"></a>

### 8.4 The numbers

- **OKUP 2025:** of 1,084 migrants studied, 51% travelled on so-called free visas <sup>[N189](#n189)</sup>.
- **Kuwait:** visa trading and airfares push costs to 5 to 6 times the government rate of Tk 1.06 lakh <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.
- **Qatar:** about 473,000 Bangladeshis work there, about 30% on development projects, Qatar's labour minister said on his visit to Dhaka from 17 May 2026 <sup>[N178](#n178)</sup>; 107,598 workers went from Bangladesh to Qatar in 2023, by the overseas employment ministry's data <sup>[N178](#n178)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-get-help-3"></a>

### 8.5 Where to get help

BMET asks anyone handed such a visa to have it and the other papers checked at BMET in Dhaka or the District Employment and Manpower Office; after it goes wrong, anyone cheated can complain to the government under the 2013 Act (see [Where victims and families get help](#where-victims-and-families-get-help)) <sup>[30](#o30), [2](#o2)</sup>.

<a id="malaysia-boats-and-land-crossings"></a>

## 9 Malaysia: boats and land crossings

<a id="how-it-is-sold-6"></a>

### 9.1 How it is sold

About 17,777 workers with BMET clearance failed to reach Malaysia before the 31 May 2024 deadline <sup>[N191 (2024)](#n191)</sup>.

**By land:** a Bangladeshi smuggling ring broken up in Malaysia in July 2026 is believed to have charged up to RM15,000 (Malaysian ringgit) per person and made about RM3.2 million since January 2026 <sup>[N8](#n8)</sup>. A group caught in Thailand in April 2026 had paid brokers Tk 7 lakh each <sup>[N7](#n7)</sup>.

**By sea, to Rohingya in the camps:** brokers lure Rohingya refugees from the Cox's Bazar camps onto trawlers with good jobs in Malaysia, and girls with a promise of marriage there, taking Tk 20,000 to 30,000 a head before boarding, police and local representatives said in October 2024 <sup>[N192 (2024)](#n192)</sup>. In April 2025 police said the people on one seized trawler had been promised good jobs in Malaysia or marriage to a suitable man, many girls had been married over the phone, and most had paid Tk 30,000 to 50,000 <sup>[N193](#n193)</sup>. Researchers of North South University's Centre for Migration Studies wrote in January 2024 that Rohingya men already in Malaysia persuade parents in the camps to marry them their young daughters without dowry on the promise that the wife can then go safely to Malaysia, and use Facebook, Imo and WhatsApp to draw young women; some who set out die at sea, some are sold as slaves in Myanmar, and some who reach Malaysia are sold there and cut off from their families <sup>[N194 (2024)](#n194)</sup>. The same researchers wrote that traffickers use the family and friends of Rohingya already in Malaysia to persuade others in the camps to go, with promises of help after arrival <sup>[N194 (2024)](#n194)</sup>.

**By sea, to Bangladeshis:** a camp-in-charge of the Refugee Relief and Repatriation Commission (RRRC, the government office that runs the Rohingya camps) said in June 2026 that Bangladeshis as well as Rohingya take the sea route, especially teenage girls taken to Malaysia and Indonesia with the lure of a "better husband" <sup>[N195](#n195)</sup>. Local brokers mainly target teenagers and young men, promising a job paying a lakh of taka with little outlay; people aged 18 to 35 with money are offered onward travel through Malaysia and Indonesia to Australia, the United States or New Zealand, and women are promised good jobs in hotels, motels, parlours or shopping malls, Prothom Alo reported in October 2024 <sup>[N196 (2024)](#n196)</sup>. The eight Bangladeshis rescued in a Teknaf raid on 15 October 2024 came from Patenga in Chattogram and from Teknaf, Cox's Bazar municipality, Maheshkhali, Ramu and Cox's Bazar Sadar, and the police officer-in-charge said an organised ring had gathered them for Malaysia in exchange for large sums of money <sup>[N197 (2024)](#n197)</sup>.

**"Go free, pay after you arrive":**
- Brokers target unemployed, needy young men of poor families and offer to take them to Malaysia by boat for free, saying they can pay after reaching Malaysia and promising very high wages, Parvez Siddiqui, executive director of the NGO Films for Peace Foundation, said in April 2026; the wife of one missing man, Belal Uddin, said he went because brokers told him he could go without money <sup>[N198](#n198)</sup>.
- Nur Kamal (21) of Daganbhuiyan, Feni, told Prothom Alo in November 2026 that someone added him to a WhatsApp group offering to take him to Malaysia free of cost, with payment due only after arrival; he left home on 2 November and on reaching Cox's Bazar was taken to a hut in the Teknaf hills, held under 10 to 12 armed guards and beaten <sup>[N199](#n199)</sup>.
- Kamal (a pseudonym) of Teknaf made a Tk 1 lakh deal to go to Malaysia, to be repaid after he found a good job <sup>[N196 (2024)](#n196)</sup>.

**A first payment, then extortion:** police said a house in Baharchhara, Teknaf, raided in December 2024 was a ring's den: people were gathered there through brokers with promises of a better life, jobs and, for unmarried women, marriage; those brought from the Rohingya camps were first charged Tk 20,000 each, and the ring intended to hold them and extort more money by force on the pretext of getting them to Malaysia <sup>[N200 (2024)](#n200)</sup>.

**Other lures:**

- A social-media friendship and an invitation to visit, which ends in abduction or trafficking, according to the Cox's Bazar police chief in June 2026; he said some fishermen think trafficking people pays more than fishing <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>.
- A day out: in September 2026 traffickers took five teenagers and young men from Mithachhari, Ramu, by CNG auto-rickshaw on the pretext of visiting Teknaf's Marine Drive <sup>[N201](#n201)</sup>.
- Australia by way of Malaysia: brokers offered one college student this for Tk 12 lakh <sup>[N195](#n195)</sup>. People already in Malaysia are being drawn into fraud with assurances that they will be sent on to Australia, according to BRAC's Shariful Islam Hasan in July 2026 <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>.

**The season:** traffickers are active when the Bay of Bengal is calm, from October or November to March, police and local representatives said in October 2024 <sup>[N192 (2024)](#n192)</sup>. The Deputy Commissioner of Cox's Bazar said in June 2026 that the risk of trafficking by sea rises in winter, when the sea is calm <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>. An additional superintendent of police in Cox's Bazar said in September 2025 that it stops in the monsoon, when the Bay of Bengal is rough <sup>[N202](#n202)</sup>. UNHCR and IOM said two boats that left Rakhine State in late June 2026 sailed outside the regular season, when the sea was more hostile and dangerous <sup>[N203](#n203)</sup>.

<a id="the-path-4"></a>

### 9.2 The path

**By land:**

1. <a id="the-path-4-item-1"></a>**Fly to Cambodia** <sup>[N7](#n7), [N8](#n8)</sup>.
2. <a id="the-path-4-item-2"></a>**Cross into Thailand on foot**, then be moved south in stages, kept in hotels and resorts <sup>[N7](#n7)</sup>.
3. <a id="the-path-4-item-3"></a>**Cross into Malaysia** through the Kelantan border, then wait in a transit house <sup>[N8](#n8)</sup>.

**By sea:**

1. <a id="malaysia-boats-and-land-crossings-item-1"></a>**Held in the hills:** people brought from around the country are kept in the hills and other places near Cox's Bazar, the Coast Guard's East Zone commander said in June 2026 <sup>[N195](#n195)</sup>. In 2025 rings worked in stages, bringing Rohingya out of the camps and driving them to the Kachchhapia area of Teknaf, police said <sup>[N193](#n193)</sup>.
2. <a id="malaysia-boats-and-land-crossings-item-2"></a>**Out to the boat at night:** small boats take them in the dark to a large trawler or ship; the coastline is 710 km long and a boat can be launched from any point on it <sup>[N195](#n195), [N24](#n24)</sup>. Departures were rising in October 2024 from the coasts of Teknaf, Ukhiya, Ramu, Cox's Bazar Sadar and Maheshkhali, the Anwara and Banshkhali coasts of Chattogram, and the Karnaphuli river <sup>[N192 (2024)](#n192)</sup>. Cox's Bazar's Superintendent of Police said in September 2025 that boats leave from 21 points in Teknaf, Ukhiya, Maheshkhali, Cox's Bazar Sadar and other upazilas, and that Rohingya girls are the most frequent victims <sup>[N202](#n202)</sup>.
3. <a id="malaysia-boats-and-land-crossings-item-3"></a>**Past St Martin's island through Myanmar waters to Thailand**, where passengers were promised they would be handed on and delivered to Malaysia <sup>[N193](#n193)</sup>. Centre for Migration Studies researchers wrote in January 2024 that traffickers first take Bangladeshis and Rohingya to Teknaf, then by boat to the Myanmar-Bangladesh sea border and on in small boats to Myanmar's Samila coast, where migrants pay traffickers in advance for the leg to Malaysia or Australia, in some cases more than was agreed in Teknaf; returnees put the cost of reaching Malaysia this way at Tk 4 to 5 lakh <sup>[N194 (2024)](#n194)</sup>.

Some journeys sold as going through Malaysia end in Myanmar instead: one man promised Australia by way of Malaysia later realised he had been taken into Myanmar, walked for several days over hills and was detained there <sup>[N195](#n195), [N24](#n24)</sup>. Eight Bangladeshis rescued off Teknaf in September 2026 told police they had been taken to Myanmar about a year earlier on the promise of Malaysia and kept there for a year <sup>[N204](#n204)</sup>. In September 2025 BGB (Border Guard Bangladesh) rescued two teenagers from Cox's Bazar town at Keruntali, Teknaf, who were being taken to Myanmar across the Naf river with the lure of a job, and detained a resident of the Ukhiya Rohingya camp <sup>[N205](#n205)</sup>.

The boat route is older: in 2012 to 2015 thousands tried boat journeys, and trafficking camps and mass graves were found on the Thailand-Malaysia border <sup>[N206](#n206)</sup>. BRAC's Shariful Islam Hasan, who reported on it then as a journalist, puts the number trafficked in that period at about one lakh, and said rescued Bangladeshis, many of them teenagers, were sent home in 2015 with their hands and feet in chains <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>. The first mass grave, with over 30 bodies, was found in April 2015 near Wang Kelian <sup>[N207 (2023)](#n207)</sup>. The dead had no names and their graves were marked only with numbers <sup>[N195](#n195)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-5"></a>

### 9.3 What happens

- **On the boat:** there is no food, clean water or toilet, people die and the bodies are thrown into the sea, the Coast Guard's East Zone commander said in June 2026; the torture begins once people are on board <sup>[N24](#n24), [N195](#n195)</sup>. Kamal of Teknaf said that during three months at sea passengers got plain rice, chilli and a glass of water once a day, those who died of hunger were thrown into the sea, anyone who asked for food or water was beaten, and the brokers collected money from families while torturing passengers <sup>[N196 (2024)](#n196)</sup>. The state minister for expatriates' welfare said in June 2026, from victims' and families' accounts, that brokers hold people hostage and take large sums from their families, many die on the way, and those who reach a shore are detained by police there <sup>[N195](#n195)</sup>. Reports of about 500 Rohingya deaths had come in during one week of July 2026, BRAC said <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>.
- **Ransom on the way:** before reaching Malaysia, Rohingya are held at the Thai or Myanmar border while ransom is demanded from relatives in the camps, and if that fails there are attempts to abduct family members, a Balukhali camp leader said in January 2025 <sup>[N208](#n208)</sup>.
- **Ransom without leaving Bangladesh:** every winter rings hold camp Rohingya for ransom in the name of the sea journey, and sometimes sail a big trawler around for several days, leave the passengers somewhere in Cox's Bazar and run off with the money, Teknaf's police chief said in December 2024 <sup>[N209 (2024)](#n209)</sup>. People held in a Teknaf hill den in November 2024 said they were told to call relatives for money and say they were in Myanmar, were tortured and not fed properly, and were held for eight to more than 13 days <sup>[N210 (2024)](#n210)</sup>. Organised rings bring Bangladeshis and Rohingya to the Teknaf coast with promises of high-paid jobs abroad and a better life, then lock them in secret hideouts and extract ransom through torture, the Coast Guard's Teknaf station in-charge said on 6 June 2026 <sup>[N211](#n211)</sup>.
- **Sold into the dens:** victims freed from a Teknaf den on 5 November 2026 said people were handed to the traffickers for money: S M Asaduzzaman (50) of Saltha, Faridpur, said a man he met in Kutupalong camp lured him with work and handed him over for Tk 10,000, after which he agreed a Tk 2 lakh release deal; Md Saiful Islam of Ukhiya said a broker named Nur Ali handed him over and the traffickers said they had bought him and another man for Tk 1 lakh, and that captives who tried to flee were beaten and chained <sup>[N199](#n199)</sup>.
- **Abduction for ransom:** at least 193 people, 86 of them Rohingya, were abducted in Teknaf in the year to January 2025 and at least 98 freed on ransom; police found links to trafficking and to some local residents <sup>[N208](#n208)</sup>.
- **Caught in Thailand:** 21 Bangladeshis found in a resort in Chana in April 2026 were charged with illegal entry and stay <sup>[N7](#n7)</sup>.
- **Jailed in Myanmar:** Mohammad Jamal of Cox's Bazar, who spent 11 months 19 days in a Myanmar jail after his trawler broke up in 2023, said Bangladeshi prisoners there did all the work, from growing vegetables to cooking, and were fed rice-water at about 7 am, some rice with dried chillies at 11 am and rice once more at 4 pm; the government brought 173 Bangladeshis home from Myanmar's prisons in April 2024 <sup>[N196 (2024)](#n196)</sup>.
- **Caught in Malaysia:** 15 smuggled Bangladeshis were caught in a Cheras transit house in July 2026 and taken to the Putrajaya immigration depot <sup>[N8](#n8)</sup>. Malaysia's Home Minister reported 465 deaths in immigration depots in 2021 to 2025 <sup>[N212](#n212)</sup>.
- **Survivors charged at home:** the nine survivors of a sinking in April 2026 were taken to Teknaf police; the three Rohingya were sent back to the camps after giving statements in court, and the six Bangladeshis were charged under the trafficking prevention law and sent to jail, with 10 to 15 unnamed accused added, police inspector Sukanta Chowdhury said <sup>[N198](#n198)</sup>.
- **Going home:** Malaysia's PRM 2.0 (Migrant Repatriation Programme) lets undocumented migrants leave; Bangladeshis were the largest group in it <sup>[N213](#n213), [N214](#n214)</sup>.
- **Families of the missing:** Manik Molla of Teknaf said in October 2024 that he had been looking for seven and a half years for his youngest brother Shakil, who left by sea for Malaysia at 17; about two years earlier a broker told him Shakil was in a Malaysian prison, took money from the family, and then disappeared <sup>[N196 (2024)](#n196)</sup>. Sohel, son of the widow Hasina Akter of Karachi Para village, Teknaf, boarded a boat for Malaysia and was not heard from again; the government told her he was held in a Malaysian prison, and she said the government would bring him back once all papers were submitted, Prothom Alo reported in October 2024 <sup>[N196 (2024)](#n196)</sup>.
- **Silence at home:** families who lose someone at sea or are cheated often don't want to admit it, which makes the crime hard to investigate, the Deputy Commissioner of Cox's Bazar said in June 2026 <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>.

<a id="cases"></a>

### 9.4 Cases

- 20 June 2014: Nurul Islam of Lohagara, Chattogram, lured with a well-paid job in Malaysia, said he and another man were taken to the Dariyanagar coast in Cox's Bazar and put on a ship by trawler; on the Thai coast brokers tortured them and took Tk 4 lakh ransom before handing them to a broker in Malaysia <sup>[N215 (2024)](#n215)</sup>. Malaysian police arrested him as an illegal immigrant in June 2017, and after a year in prison the government brought him home in October 2018 <sup>[N215 (2024)](#n215)</sup>. He filed a trafficking case against six people in October 2018; in 2019 the 12-year-old brother of an accused was produced in the High Court in his place and given bail, and in May 2024 the Chattogram tribunal sent the man claiming to be that accused to jail and ordered police to find out who the real accused is <sup>[N215 (2024)](#n215)</sup>.
- July 2023: Mohammad Jamal of Cox's Bazar, persuaded by a broker named Ayub to go to Malaysia on a Tk 4 lakh deal, was taken with nine others by small boat from Gorjonia in Ramu upazila to a small fishing trawler carrying 73 people, five of them girls under 18; after three days the trawler's hull split at the Myanmar border, the passengers surrendered to Myanmar's navy, and he spent 11 months 19 days in a Myanmar jail before coming home with government help, Prothom Alo reported in October 2024 <sup>[N196 (2024)](#n196)</sup>.
- 5 Jan 2024: Cox's Bazar assistant judge Maitree Bhattacharya saw three Rohingya girls (two aged 12, one 14) from Balukhali camp being put on a wooden boat for Malaysia at Lombarighat on the Teknaf Marine Drive; police with her rescued them and arrested a trafficker, and in what was reported as the first Overseas Employment and Migrants Act case against Rohingya fleeing the camps, the girls were sent to jail with the trafficker, bailed on 9 January and were to be returned to the camp, from where they must attend court <sup>[N216 (2024)](#n216)</sup>.
- Told Oct 2024: Kamal (a pseudonym) of Teknaf made a Tk 1 lakh deal with a man named Abu to go to Malaysia, to be repaid after he found a good job; brokers pressed for Tk 3 lakh ransom, Malaysian police chased the boat into Indonesian waters, the Bangladeshis on board sank the trawler after two of them died in a quarrel with Rohingya passengers, the Indonesian navy rescued them, and after 11 months' detention there the Bangladesh government brought them home, at a total cost to him of Tk 2 lakh 30 thousand <sup>[N196 (2024)](#n196)</sup>.
- 12 Oct 2024: the Coast Guard seized a trawler carrying 17 Rohingya from Teknaf's Mochhoni camp at Shikalbaha in Karnaphuli upazila, Chattogram, and arrested three members of a trafficking ring <sup>[N192 (2024)](#n192)</sup>.
- 14 Oct 2024: brokers who had put more than a hundred Rohingya from the Ukhiya camps on a fishing trawler for Malaysia set them down at dawn on Inani beach after 10 days at sea; police and the Coast Guard detained 26 and the brokers hid <sup>[N192 (2024)](#n192)</sup>. Another 80 to 90 Rohingya escaped <sup>[N197 (2024)](#n197)</sup>. One woman said she had paid a broker Tk 30,000 for a good job in Malaysia; another passenger said the trawler left Teknaf, met the Myanmar navy south of St Martin's island and was turned back <sup>[N192 (2024)](#n192)</sup>.
- 15 Oct 2024: late at night Teknaf police raided a house in Dakshin Lambari, Teknaf Sadar union, and rescued 20 people gathered to be trafficked to Malaysia by sea, 12 Rohingya including women and children and 8 Bangladeshis, and detained three brokers, Luna Begum and Md Riduan of Dakshin Lambari and Md Abdullah of Kachubunia, Sabrang union, the Teknaf Model police station's officer-in-charge, Muhammad Gias Uddin, said <sup>[N197 (2024)](#n197)</sup>.
- 12 to 17 Nov 2024: Ekhlas Mia of Ukhiya and his cousin were abducted on the Marine Drive and held in a Teknaf hilltop den with men, women and children tortured for money; unable to pay, he was taken to the beach with 15 to 20 others to be sent by sea, slipped away and asked RAB (Rapid Action Battalion, an elite police force) to rescue the rest <sup>[N210 (2024)](#n210)</sup>. RAB rescued 31 people, 27 Rohingya and 4 Bangladeshis, from the den in the Kachchhapia hills; each had paid Tk 20,000 for the sea journey, the women promised good grooms and the men good jobs <sup>[N210 (2024)](#n210)</sup>. Two men were arrested and a case filed under the trafficking law <sup>[N210 (2024)](#n210)</sup>.
- 14 Dec 2024: Teknaf police rescued 30 Rohingya, 15 men, 3 women and 12 children, held for ransom in a house in Dakshin Lambari Para after being gathered for Malaysia with promises of jobs and marriage; the ring fled and nobody was arrested <sup>[N209 (2024)](#n209)</sup>.
- 29 Dec 2024: at about 4 am police raided Abdul Amin's house on a hilltop in Moddhom Kachchhapia, Baharchhara union, Teknaf, rescued 66 people gathered to be trafficked to Malaysia by sea (18 men, 11 women and 37 children; 5 Bangladeshis and 61 Rohingya from the camps), detained five members of the ring and recovered four rifle bullets, a ramdao and a kirich (machete and blade) <sup>[N200 (2024)](#n200)</sup>.
- 20 Jan 2025: at midnight the Navy and police rescued 19 people, two Bangladeshis and 17 Rohingya from the Ukhiya-Teknaf camps, gathered in huts at the house of Zafar Alam in the hilly Madhyam Kachchhapia area of Baharchhara union, Teknaf, to be trafficked to Malaysia by sea; Zafar Alam (30) was caught after a chase, the others fled, and a case was being prepared, the Teknaf officer-in-charge said <sup>[N217](#n217)</sup>.
- 25 Jan 2025: the Bangladesh Navy raided a traffickers' den in the Kachchhopia hills, Baharchhara union, Teknaf, rescued 17 men, women and children held hostage for trafficking to Malaysia by sea, 3 of them Bangladeshis and the rest Rohingya from the Ukhiya and Teknaf camps, and arrested a trafficker named Ali Abdullah, Lieutenant Commander Afzal Hossain of the Navy's Teknaf contingent said <sup>[N218](#n218)</sup>.
- 2 to 9 Apr 2025: an auto-rickshaw driver from Ukhiya said two passengers held him at gunpoint, took him to the Kachchhapia hills, kept him four days in a hut with 20 to 25 others and forced him onto a trawler on 7 April <sup>[N193](#n193)</sup>. On 8 April the navy seized that trawler, FB Kulsuma, 44 nautical miles south-west of St Martin's island with 214 people aboard, 118 men, 68 women and 28 children, and no life-saving equipment or enough food and water; it had left Shaplapur beach in Teknaf <sup>[N193](#n193)</sup>. The RRRC said action was being taken against 12 of the Rohingya for various crimes and the rest went back to the camps <sup>[N193](#n193)</sup>. A woman married by phone was among them (see Marriage, family-visa and fake-nikahnama schemes) <sup>[N193](#n193)</sup>.
- 4 Jul 2025: four related schoolboys and madrasa students aged 14 to 16 from Mandalpara, Fatekharkul union, Ramu, went missing; their families, who filed four missing-person diaries at Ramu police station the next day, suspected traffickers or kidnappers, and on 6 July the Ramu officer-in-charge said police had been told the boys were in a house in Bangla Bazar, Jhilongja union, Cox's Bazar Sadar, and were working to rescue them <sup>[N219](#n219)</sup>.
- Oct 2025: BGB rescued 29 Rohingya from the Marine Drive coast of Teknaf who were going by sea through brokers to Malaysia and Thailand, and detained three brokers, Prothom Alo reported on 23 October 2025 <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.
- 26 Mar to 9 Apr 2026: UNHCR (the UN refugee agency) said an overloaded boat that left Bangladesh on 26 March 2026 sank in rough sea with about 250 people missing, and only nine were rescued alive near the Andaman Islands on 9 April <sup>[N221](#n221)</sup>. The nine survivors of the sinking, which Prothom Alo dates to 8 April, were picked up from water drums and pieces of wood by the Bangladesh-flagged merchant ship Meghna Pride and handed to the Coast Guard; the six Bangladeshis among them were then jailed <sup>[N198](#n198)</sup>.
- 23 Apr 2026: at about 11 pm the Coast Guard's Teknaf station detained Md Yasin (28) of Maheshkhaliapara, Teknaf, with a 7.65 mm foreign pistol; the Coast Guard said he admitted long involvement in trafficking and faces several murder and trafficking cases <sup>[N222](#n222)</sup>.
- 30 Mar 2026: the Coast Guard raided a house in Dakshin Lombori, Teknaf, which it called a traffickers' den, and rescued seven people including women and children who had been brought there, with promises of a better life, high-paid jobs and a cheap passage, to be taken to Malaysia by sea; the traffickers fled and the seven were handed to Teknaf Model Police Station <sup>[N223](#n223)</sup>.
- 4 Apr 2026: seven young men from Hajipara, Miapara and Natun Ghona villages of Rajakhali union, Pekua, Cox's Bazar, left the Teknaf coast on one of three trawlers that left for Malaysia at about the same time, phoned home until 8 April and have not been heard from since; the Teknaf brokers stopped answering their phones, and one missing man's uncle said Teknaf police only took photos and addresses and sent the family away <sup>[N198](#n198)</sup>.
- 5 Jun 2026: the Coast Guard chased and seized a fishing trawler off Baharchhara on the Teknaf coast that was taking people to Malaysia by sea, rescued 50 people including women and children (39 Bangladeshis and 11 Rohingya) and arrested nine traffickers, who were handed to Teknaf Model Police Station with a case filed, station in-charge Lt Cdr Md Muttakin Siddiqui said <sup>[N211](#n211)</sup>.
- Told June 2026: Tarekur Rahman of Maheshkhali said a broker promising an easy Malaysian visa took him to Myanmar, where he was beaten and threatened with death and Tk 1 lakh was taken from his family <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>. In the first report of his account, a further Tk 7 lakh was taken in Thailand and he was caught at the border on the way to Malaysia, jailed and sent home <sup>[N195](#n195)</sup>. In the fuller account, his family sold their house under torture, food sometimes came only after three or four days, and after another large payment for the onward leg he was caught near the border and held about seven months in a Myanmar prison <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>.
- Told June 2026: Aung Aung U Jewel, a college student who could not afford the language tests needed to go legally, set off when brokers promised a cheap route to Australia by way of Malaysia for Tk 12 lakh; they took him into Myanmar, where after days of walking over hills he was detained and spent a month on remand and several months in prison <sup>[N195](#n195), [N24](#n24)</sup>. His family had no news of him until he sent his mother a letter through a Myanmar police officer, and the brokers took money from the family as a "rescue cost" <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>. When fighting began while he was held in Thandwe prison the prisoners were moved and made to work, and he later came home with the Bangladesh embassy's help <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>.
- Late June to 8 Jul 2026: UNHCR and IOM said on 16 July 2026 that two boats carrying more than 500 passengers, mostly Rohingya and including Rohingya from the refugee camps in Bangladesh, left Myanmar's Rakhine State in late June; contact with one, carrying about 250 people, was lost soon after it set out, and the other, carrying about 280, was believed to have sunk near Myanmar's Irrawaddy coast on 8 July <sup>[N203](#n203)</sup>. The UN feared more than 500 had died; the two agencies said the incidents and the death toll were not yet officially confirmed <sup>[N203](#n203)</sup>. Abdul Kuddus, a Prothom Alo staff reporter, said on 26 July 2026 that a trawler carrying more than 280 people from the Teknaf coast had sunk in the Andaman Sea, that foreign agencies reported the sinking first, and that Teknaf journalists had not known people were being loaded <sup>[N224](#n224)</sup>.
- 11 to 20 Sep 2026: traffickers took five teenagers and young men (two aged 16, two 18 and one 20) from Mithachhari, Ramu, by CNG auto-rickshaw on the pretext of visiting Teknaf's Marine Drive, blindfolded and held them, beat them and collected ransom from their families in instalments while planning to send them by sea to Indonesia and Malaysia; SRB-15 (Special Response Battalion 15) rescued them on 20 September at Khonkarpara ghat near Sabrang Zero Point, Teknaf, and arrested Md Anas alias Munna (21) <sup>[N201](#n201)</sup>.
- 22 Sep 2026: a boat of irregular migrants from Myanmar's Rakhine state capsized off the Baharchhara coast of Teknaf at dawn, killing a Rohingya woman, Chhura Khatun (28), and her 9-year-old son; of the 16 rescued, eight were Bangladeshis who told police they had been taken to Myanmar about a year earlier on the promise of Malaysia, kept in various parts of Myanmar for a year, and put on the boat for Malaysia the night before <sup>[N204](#n204)</sup>.
- 1 Oct 2026: the navy ship BNS Khalid Bin Walid, alerted by fishermen, rescued 76 people from a wooden boat whose engine had failed and which was sinking about 81 km west of the St Martin's Island lighthouse; the navy said 65 men, women and children aboard, some lured with good jobs and some kidnapped and held in remote parts of Teknaf, were being trafficked to Malaysia by way of the Myanmar and Thai coasts, and it detained 11 alleged traffickers <sup>[N225](#n225)</sup>.
- 5 Nov 2026: a joint army, BGB (Border Guard Bangladesh) and police operation freed 57 people from a traffickers' den in the deep hills of Rangikhali, Teknaf, where they were being tortured for ransom and were to be put on a trawler to Malaysia that night; a soldier, Md Titas Sheikh (27), was shot dead in a gunfight, five ring members were detained, and police said one of the 57 was identified as a trafficker <sup>[N199](#n199)</sup>. According to ISPR (the armed forces' public relations directorate), intelligence sources had reported about 120 Rohingya and Bangladeshis held hostage there by an armed group of 15 to 16 traffickers, who fired indiscriminately when the joint team of the army's 10th Infantry Division, BGB and police arrived at about 5 pm; the army said a pistol, a locally made gun and four bullets were recovered <sup>[N226](#n226)</sup>.
- 7 Nov 2026: BGB rescued nine people, including two aged 16 and 17, gathered at South Lombori ghat, Teknaf Sadar union, to be taken to Malaysia by sea, while 10 to 15 ring members fled by boat; the rescued said men were lured with good jobs and women with marriage to suitable grooms in Malaysia, the Teknaf BGB battalion commander said <sup>[N227](#n227)</sup>.
