- 16 to 20 Nov 2026: 72 people, mostly Rohingya, who had paid brokers Tk 10,000 to 15,000 each, were taken by small fishing boats from Mithapanir Chhara ghat, Teknaf, to a large wooden trawler; after rough seas from a cyclone kept it near St Martin's Island and the crew and brokers quarrelled, the crew landed them at Laboni Point on Cox's Bazar beach late on 20 November and fled, and police rescued 28, mostly Rohingya and the rest from Teknaf, Naikhongchhari in Bandarban and other districts, while the rest ran away <sup>[N228](#n228)</sup>.
- 21 to 22 Nov 2026: police said 70 to 80 men and women had been gathered at a traffickers' den at Lengurbil, Teknaf Sadar union, to be sent by sea; when a joint forces operation led by the army's 10th Infantry Division, Ramu, went in at about 12:30 am, the traffickers fired on the forces while escaping by boat and took most of the people with them, and 5 women, 2 men and 1 child were rescued <sup>[N229](#n229)</sup>. Teknaf Model police station's officer-in-charge, Md Saiful Islam, said the eight were handed to the station and what to do with them would be decided once their identities were confirmed <sup>[N229](#n229)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Did the boat that sank near the Andaman Islands leave Bangladesh on 26 March 2026, as UNHCR said, or was it one of the trawlers that left Teknaf on 4 April and sank on 8 April <sup>[N221](#n221), [N198](#n198)</sup>?

<a id="the-numbers-5"></a>

### 9.5 The numbers

- **2016 to 2023:** law enforcers rescued 1,134 people being taken to Malaysia by sea, 95% of them Rohingya; 85 cases were filed at Ukhiya and Teknaf police stations naming 1,200 accused, of whom 508 were caught <sup>[N192 (2024)](#n192)</sup>. Police and Rohingya leaders said in January 2024 that in the previous five years law enforcers had rescued at least 5,000 Rohingya being trafficked to Malaysia by trawler from the Teknaf, Ukhiya, Cox's Bazar Sadar and Maheshkhali coasts, and that none of those rescued had been charged or jailed <sup>[N216 (2024)](#n216)</sup>.
- **2018 to 2022:** by UNHCR's figures, cited by researchers of the Centre for Migration Studies in January 2024 and by Prothom Alo in October 2024, 8,312 Rohingya refugees tried to reach Malaysia by sea, and 589 of them died at sea <sup>[N194 (2024)](#n194), [N196 (2024)](#n196)</sup>.
- **2024:** sea trafficking from Teknaf rose again from January 2024 after six or seven years of being largely stopped, and in the year to January 2025 at least 600 people, including 45 traffickers, were caught in eight or nine operations on the Teknaf coast <sup>[N208](#n208)</sup>. From Lambashia camp alone more than 500 young men and women were sent towards Malaysia in that year, and more than 300 were caught and brought back, a camp leader said <sup>[N208](#n208)</sup>.
- **January 2025:** Baharchhara union parishad chairman Amjad Hossain said that trafficking syndicates bring Rohingya from the Ukhiya-Teknaf camps to several points on the Baharchhara coast, lure them with promises of Malaysia, hold them in the hills and demand ransom, and that police, RAB and Navy operations had rescued about 150 such victims in January 2025 alone <sup>[N218](#n218)</sup>.
- **2023 to 2024 (BNWLA):** Safayet Bin Kamal of BNWLA (the Bangladesh National Women Lawyers' Association) said in September 2025 that 137 trafficking cases were recorded nationwide in 2023, with 44% of the victims from Cox's Bazar district, that 86 trafficking incidents occurred in Cox's Bazar in July to September 2024, most of the victims Rohingya, and that more than 657 Rohingya died on 150 dangerous sea voyages in 2024 <sup>[N202](#n202)</sup>.
- **2024 to 2025 (IOM):** IOM's Cox's Bazar office said in September 2025 that it had identified more than 600 trafficking incidents in the Ukhiya-Teknaf camps in the past year that could have gone to court, but that no cases were being filed (see [Why cases fail](#why-cases-fail)) <sup>[N202](#n202)</sup>.
- **2025:** about 900 Rohingya refugees went missing or died in the Andaman Sea and Bay of Bengal, the deadliest year on record for sea journeys in South and South-East Asia, with one in seven of the more than 6,500 who attempted the crossing dying or going missing, according to UNHCR as reported by Prothom Alo on 17 April 2026 <sup>[N221](#n221)</sup>.
- **2026:** UNHCR said that more than half of the Rohingya crossing by sea in recent years were women and children, that more than 2,800 had taken the journey in 2026 up to 13 April, and that the boats usually leave Cox's Bazar in Bangladesh or Rakhine State in Myanmar for Indonesia or Malaysia <sup>[N221](#n221)</sup>. By mid-July 2026 about 300 Rohingya refugees and Bangladeshi citizens had been reported missing or dead in the Andaman Sea and Bay of Bengal that year, according to UNHCR and IOM <sup>[N203](#n203)</sup>.
- **Deaths and missing:** at least 300 people died in more than 20 trawler sinkings in the years to October 2024, according to Abdus Shukkur, a Cox's Bazar human rights lawyer, who said the rings especially target Rohingya girls <sup>[N192 (2024)](#n192)</sup>. About 5,787 people went missing on illegal sea journeys in 2023 alone, about 25% of them women and children and half of them Bangladeshi citizens, according to an assistant representative of UNHCR (the UN refugee agency) in June 2026 <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>. About two lakh people have crossed the Bay of Bengal in the past decade and many have gone missing, according to BRAC's Shariful Islam Hasan <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported on 25 July 2026 that trafficking by sea through the Teknaf-Ukhiya coast was still increasing and happening almost every day, and an Ukhiya police sub-inspector said that day that every year many people from the Cox's Bazar area die or go missing on this route and their families are left destitute <sup>[N230](#n230)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What does UNHCR's own publication give for people missing at sea on the Bay of Bengal route in 2023, and how many were women and children?
> - How many people were rescued from Malaysia-bound boats in 2016 to 2023: 1,134, or at least 5,000 Rohingya in the five years to January 2024 <sup>[N192 (2024)](#n192), [N216 (2024)](#n216)</sup>?

<a id="the-law-5"></a>

### 9.6 The law

- **Malaysia:** anyone who entered unlawfully or holds forged travel documents is a prohibited immigrant <sup>[32 (2006)](#o32)</sup>. Smuggling cases are brought under the Anti-Trafficking in Persons and Anti-Smuggling of Migrants Act 2007, the Immigration Act and the Passports Act <sup>[N8](#n8)</sup>.
- **Thailand:** in 2017 Thailand convicted 62 people, including nine officials, over the deaths and trafficking of Rohingya and Bangladeshis to Malaysia <sup>[N207 (2023)](#n207)</sup>.
- **Bangladesh, against passengers:** in January 2024 police in Cox's Bazar said that Rohingya who flee the Ukhiya camps and try to go to Malaysia or another country by sea would from then on be charged under the Overseas Employment and Migrants Act and sent to jail <sup>[N216 (2024)](#n216)</sup>. Three Rohingya girls rescued that month were jailed with the trafficker, and six Bangladeshi survivors of an April 2026 sinking were charged under the trafficking prevention law and jailed <sup>[N216 (2024)](#n216), [N198](#n198)</sup>.
- **Bangladesh, in practice:** even when cases are filed, traffickers often get out of jail and go back to the same work, the Coast Guard's East Zone commander said in June 2026 <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>. Cox's Bazar's 462 trafficking cases from 2012 to August 2025 were pending in three tribunals (see [What happens to trafficking cases](#what-happens-to-trafficking-cases)) <sup>[N202](#n202)</sup>. The district's police chief said in September 2025 that joint operations regularly rescue people and arrest traffickers and that trafficking had fallen somewhat but could not be brought under control; an additional superintendent said there was no research on why it happens or who is involved, no updated list of traffickers, and 5,000 fishing trawlers at sea <sup>[N202](#n202)</sup>. C R Abrar of RMMRU said in July 2024 that "big fish" are said to be behind trafficking across the Bay of Bengal but are not identified <sup>[N20 (2024)](#n20)</sup>. Twenty abduction cases were filed at Teknaf police station in the year to January 2025, and its officer-in-charge said victims released on ransom do not want to complain <sup>[N208](#n208)</sup>. The Home Minister, Salahuddin Ahmed, said in Teknaf on 14 April 2026 that trafficking by illegal routes had increased, that Teknaf, Ukhiya and some areas of Cox's Bazar had been identified as risky, and that the departure points would be identified and special operations run <sup>[N231](#n231)</sup>. At a workshop on 26 July 2026 Teknaf Model police station's officer-in-charge called Teknaf a "red zone" for trafficking and smuggling, and a police additional deputy commissioner, Momena Akter, said trafficking money stays abroad, strengthens the gangs and buys drugs that come back into Bangladesh <sup>[N224](#n224)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-get-help-4"></a>

### 9.7 Where to get help

Bangladesh High Commission, Kuala Lumpur: No 8, Lorong Yap Kwan Seng, 50450 Kuala Lumpur; consular +60326040949 <sup>[33](#o33)</sup>. In Thailand: the Bangladesh Embassy in Bangkok (see [Where victims and families get help](#where-victims-and-families-get-help)) <sup>[34](#o34)</sup>. A "Digital Island" project on Maheshkhali, launched in 2017 with IOM funding at a cost of about Tk 22.36 crore, whose aims included stopping unsafe migration and which let law enforcement use high-speed internet for sea operations against trafficking, ran for about two and a half years and then stalled; Prothom Alo found in September 2025 that its equipment had rotted and 19 km of optical fibre had been stolen <sup>[N232](#n232)</sup>. In Myanmar: Tarekur Rahman said he got no real help from it in prison and came home on money his family sent, while Aung Aung U Jewel came home with its help <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>. BRAC's Migration Programme, with the Australian government's support, ran a four-day campaign in July 2026 under the slogan "No going abroad by sea", with events in Gazipur, Dhaka and Narayanganj and, on 25 July, a 100 km road show from Ukhiya through Teknaf and along the Marine Drive to Kolatoli on Cox's Bazar beach; a BRAC manager said there that Rohingya in the camps are being trafficked through various lures <sup>[N230](#n230)</sup>. Detained migrants have been brought home by the government from Myanmar's prisons (173 in April 2024) and from Indonesian detention <sup>[N196 (2024)](#n196)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What does the Cambodia-Thailand-Malaysia land route cost in late 2026?
> - Which papers must a family submit, and to which office, for the government to bring home a relative held in a prison in Malaysia, Myanmar or Indonesia after a boat journey <sup>[N196 (2024)](#n196)</sup>?

<a id="india-women-and-girls-taken-across-the-land-border"></a>

## 10 India: women and girls taken across the land border

<a id="how-it-is-sold-7"></a>

### 10.1 How it is sold

Traffickers usually target women from low-income families in financial trouble <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.

**Offers in reported cases:**

- a job in India, ending in a brothel <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>;
- a job in Canada <sup>[N234 (2024)](#n234)</sup>;
- TikTok: a ring trafficked women to India "in the name of TikTok" <sup>[N234 (2024)](#n234)</sup>; Dipta Rakshit, Winrock International's country representative, said in July 2024 that many more "Hridoys" had since taken the place of "TikToker Hridoy" and that educated people were also falling into their traps <sup>[N20 (2024)](#n20)</sup>;
- blackmail online: traffickers misuse TikTok and other apps, trapping people with threats to spread a love affair or private photos and videos and extorting money, A K M Masud Ali, executive director of the NGO INCIDIN Bangladesh, said in July 2024 <sup>[N20 (2024)](#n20)</sup>;
- learning dance in India with a salary of Tk 50,000 a month, offered in January 2025 to a class-eight girl from the Facebook account of a girl she knew, whose mother used the same account and helped three girls set off <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>;
- cheaper cancer treatment in India and work there to pay for it, offered by a woman neighbour <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>;
- work through a cousin the relatives believed was a domestic worker in Maharashtra <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>;
- higher-paid parlour work in India, offered to women garment workers in Chattogram, especially those who had lost factory jobs, by a woman who befriended them <sup>[N235 (2024)](#n235)</sup>;
- a love affair and marriage (see Marriage, family-visa and fake-nikahnama schemes) <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.

<a id="the-path-5"></a>

### 10.2 The path

1. <a id="the-path-5-item-1"></a>**A relay of handlers:** in February 2025 three girls took a bus from Dhaka to Jhenaidah; the conductor handed them to a man, who took them to a woman's house, and that evening a battery auto-rickshaw driver took them to a man at the Maheshpur border <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>. Each person has a separate task, so victims cannot say who took them from where, a Border Guard Bangladesh (BGB) battalion commander in Jashore said <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
2. <a id="the-path-5-item-2"></a>**The crossing:** routes run through Putkhali, Daulatpur, Gatipara, Sadipur and Rudrapur around Benapole in Jashore, through Maheshpur in Jhenaidah and the Satkhira border, with scattered cases through Tamabil in Sylhet and the Cumilla border <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>. The Deputy Commissioner of Mymensingh said in January 2025 that trafficking also happens through that district's two border upazilas (sub-districts), Haluaghat and Dhobaura <sup>[N236](#n236)</sup>.
3. <a id="the-path-5-item-3"></a>**Sold:** rescued women have been found in brothels in Hyderabad, Maharashtra and Bengaluru <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.

**The prices:** traffickers take Tk 15,000 to 20,000 a person just to get someone across, far more than before because checks have tightened, and rescued women said they were told in the brothels that they had been bought for Tk 2 to 3 lakh, according to the director of Rights Jashore, a local rights organisation, in early 2025 <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-6"></a>

### 10.3 What happens

- **Rescued in India:** the Indian Border Security Force (BSF) hands the person to the BGB and immigration police at the "zero line" of the Benapole border <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
- **Jailed in India:** a 28-year-old Savar woman promised Canada was trafficked to Nadia district, West Bengal, and came home after seven months in jail there <sup>[N234 (2024)](#n234)</sup>.
- **Caught crossing, then charged at home:** in the cases below, the BSF handed people it caught crossing into India back to the BGB at a flag meeting, and the BGB handed them to the local police for legal action <sup>[N237 (2024)](#n237), [N238](#n238), [N239](#n239)</sup>.
  - 28 August 2024: four Bangladeshis who crossed at the Boroshoshi border, Boda, Panchagarh, at night were detained by the BSF, returned the next day and handed to Boda police <sup>[N237 (2024)](#n237)</sup>.
  - 13 February 2025: two young men from Ranisankail, Thakurgaon, caught after entering India through the Betna border in Haripur were returned and handed to Haripur police, whose officer-in-charge said a case had been filed against them and they would be sent to court the next day <sup>[N238](#n238)</sup>.
  - 25 February 2025: seven Bangladeshis, among them a man, his wife and their 21 and 17-year-old sons, crossed the Mahananda river through an unfenced stretch of the Kashimganj border, Tetulia, Panchagarh, at night and were detained by the BSF; after their return the BGB filed a case against all seven under the Passport Act for illegal entry, and police were taking steps to produce them in court <sup>[N239](#n239)</sup>.

<a id="cases-1"></a>

### 10.4 Cases

- 2010 to 2025: a 19-year-old woman who eloped with and married Md Shahinur Rahman alias Sabuj Sheikh on 5 August 2010 was found in November 2010 in Dum Dum Central Jail, Kolkata, after police rescued her from a brothel, and later killed herself in that jail <sup>[N240](#n240)</sup>. Madhukhali police would not take a case under the trafficking law, so her father filed it at the Faridpur Women and Children Repression Prevention Tribunal on 24 December 2010; on 30 September 2025, 15 years later, the tribunal sentenced the absconding Shahinur to life imprisonment and a Tk 50,000 fine <sup>[N240](#n240)</sup>.
- 2021: a young woman trafficked by the TikTok ring escaped back to Bangladesh on 7 May 2021 and on 1 June filed a case at Hatirjheel police station naming 12 people, among them Rifadul Islam alias Hridoy (26), "TikTok Hridoy" <sup>[N234 (2024)](#n234)</sup>. Three years later the Bangladesh investigation was still unfinished, although accused arrested in India over the same events had been tried and punished there; Salma Ali of BNWLA (Bangladesh National Woman Lawyers' Association) blamed the lack of accountability of investigators <sup>[N234 (2024)](#n234)</sup>.
- 29 May to 24 Jun 2024: according to her case statement, a Chattogram garment worker and a friend went to Jashore with Md Tarek on 29 May, saw the border fence six kilometres on, were kept in a room beside the border, and on 2 June were taken across the fence to a hotel in Jharkhand, from which they escaped <sup>[N235 (2024)](#n235)</sup>. One filed a case at Bayezid Bostami police station naming Tarek, his wife Jhumu Akter and their associate Anisur Rahman, and police arrested Tarek on 24 June; police said women were held in hotels there and forced into sex work <sup>[N235 (2024)](#n235)</sup>.
- 10 Oct 2024: a 22-year-old divorced woman in Dhaka with cancer was trafficked to India after a neighbour's offer; Hyderabad police rescued her from a brothel on 21 November <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
- Told Feb 2025: a 22-year-old woman from near the border in Jashore said she was taken to India on the promise of a job and sold to a brothel, where she saw three Bangladeshi girls; Indian police rescued her two years later and BNWLA rehabilitated her <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
- 10 Feb 2025: BGB members chased the three Dhaka schoolgirls and the man taking them across the border at Maheshpur, Jhenaidah; one girl fell, all three were rescued, and the man, his face wrapped in a gamcha (cotton towel), escaped <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>. One girl filed a case at Maheshpur police station on 24 February; nobody had been arrested by 8 March <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
- 11 Feb 2025: a 15-year-old girl from a poor family was trafficked after her cousin's offer; Maharashtra police rescued her on 23 February, and Justice and Care, an anti-trafficking organisation, said she had been used for sex work <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.

<a id="the-numbers-6"></a>

### 10.5 The numbers

- **Border Guard Bangladesh:** from August 2024 to January 2025, 145 people, traffickers included, were detained while being taken across illegally, 55 of them women and children, and 162 people were brought back from India <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
- **Justice and Care:** in January 2024 to February 2025, 186 women and children were brought back from India and 133 rescued at the border, counting only BGB cases in the Jhenaidah, Chuadanga and Jashore areas <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>. The return of at least 10 women and girls rescued from brothels was in process in February 2025, Justice and Care said <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
- **Bangladesh overall:** a UN global report on trafficking published in 2024 counted 452 women (228 of them trafficked abroad) and 63 girls trafficked from Bangladesh from April 2023 to March 2024, more than in earlier years <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-6"></a>

### 10.6 The law

Taking someone across the border by deceit for sexual exploitation is trafficking under the 2026 Act (see "File a trafficking case" in the Dalal scams doc) <sup>[35](#o35)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-get-help-5"></a>

### 10.7 Where to get help

- **BNWLA** rehabilitated the Jashore woman rescued from an Indian brothel <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
- **Justice and Care** was handling the return of at least 10 women and girls rescued from Indian brothels in February 2025 <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>. Its country director, Tariqul Islam, said in July 2024 that because the two countries have different laws, rescuing a victim of cross-border trafficking takes a long time <sup>[N20 (2024)](#n20)</sup>.

<a id="cambodia-myanmar-and-laos-scam-compounds"></a>

## 11 Cambodia, Myanmar and Laos: scam compounds

<a id="how-it-is-sold-8"></a>

### 11.1 How it is sold

The job is "computer operator", "data entry", "call centre" or "restaurant" work, for about Tk 5.3 to 6 lakh <sup>[N19](#n19), [N158](#n158), [N15](#n15), [N241](#n241)</sup>. A data-entry job was promised at USD 800 a month <sup>[N15](#n15)</sup>. CID says traffickers advertise these jobs through fake websites, email, Facebook, WhatsApp and Telegram <sup>[N242](#n242)</sup>. Bangladeshis already working in the Gulf, including on a ship in Dubai, are recruited too with promises of higher pay <sup>[N243](#n243), [N242](#n242)</sup>. Many went with BMET clearance <sup>[N18](#n18), [N19](#n19)</sup>. A BRAC study found 66% of cyber-scam victims were recruited through middlemen and 98% never got the promised job <sup>[N149](#n149)</sup>. Of 473 people back from Cambodian scam centres, 80% had gone through a recruiting agency in the regular process, the Bangladesh representative of Winrock International, a development organisation, said in July 2026 <sup>[N244](#n244)</sup>.

People in the sector say Cambodia's economy is no better than Bangladesh's and it has few jobs, yet workers are sent there for the scam centres <sup>[N99](#n99), [N245](#n245)</sup>. With the Middle East market shrinking, people who are a little better off have been going to countries such as Russia and Cambodia, where many are cheated, Prothom Alo reported in October 2026 <sup>[N245](#n245)</sup>. Volunteers of migration organisations said in November 2026 that people are also held in scam compounds in Vietnam <sup>[N153](#n153)</sup>.

<a id="the-path-6"></a>

### 11.2 The path

- **Cambodia:** the worker flies in on a one-month visit visa, not a work visa; Bangladeshi brokers meet him at the airport, keep him briefly in a hotel and sell him to a guarded compound, often Chinese-run <sup>[N246](#n246), [N3](#n3), [N247](#n247)</sup>. Compounds are in Sihanoukville, along the Thai and Lao borders, and in rural areas <sup>[N248](#n248)</sup>. Many of about 16,000 who went in 18 months were shown an employment visa before leaving and later given a tourist visa and sold, although they had followed the government's rules, according to the president of Anirban Survivor Voice Foundation, an organisation led by trafficking survivors <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>. One returnee was kept three days in a Phnom Penh restaurant, then taken late at night to a scam centre for an "interview"; the people who sent him said this was the work and he would later be sent on to Europe <sup>[N45](#n45)</sup>.
- **Myanmar and Laos:** victims are taken across the Thai border into Myanmar, Laos or Cambodia; Myawaddy on the Thai-Myanmar border is a hub <sup>[N249](#n249), [N250](#n250)</sup>. Eight Bangladeshis who went to Cambodia and Laos for work in 2025 worked there for a time, then were taken by road through Thailand and sold to a scam centre in Myanmar, according to Anirban Survivor Voice Foundation <sup>[N251](#n251)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-7"></a>

### 11.3 What happens

- **Sold:** men are sold to compounds for USD 1,500 to 10,000; one man was sold to three compounds in 2022 <sup>[N3](#n3), [N252](#n252), [N249](#n249)</sup>.
- **Forced to scam:** they are held at gunpoint and forced to take part in the scams <sup>[N242](#n242)</sup>. Those who miss daily targets are beaten or given electric shocks <sup>[N4](#n4)</sup>.
- **Ransom:** a compound demanded USD 5,000 to release two youths; another family was pushed to pay Tk 95,000 more <sup>[N252](#n252), [N253](#n253)</sup>.
- **Abandoned:** a man who escaped after three months paid his own ticket and hotel home <sup>[N241](#n241)</sup>.
- **Detained leaving:** on 3 September 2026 Cambodia's immigration police detained 32 Bangladeshis at Phnom Penh airport as they tried to fly home, and held 30 of them in an immigration detention centre <sup>[N254](#n254)</sup>. They had gone under the return process set by the Cambodian government but were held for illegal stay <sup>[N254](#n254)</sup>. They had been taken there by a broker ring with the lure of a good salary, many were forced into cyber scamming, and those who tried to escape were allegedly tortured <sup>[N254](#n254)</sup>. They landed in Dhaka on 20 October 2026 on Thai Airways TG-321, after being held since 3 September <sup>[N255](#n255)</sup>.
- **Escaping into Thailand:** eight Bangladeshis who escaped a Myanmar compound into Thailand had no visa, surrendered to the immigration police and were kept in a safe home on a charge of illegal entry <sup>[N251](#n251)</sup>.
- **The same rings in Bangladesh:** on 11 September 2026 police arrested 70 foreigners in a building in Jalalabad, Khulshi, Chattogram, accused of scam-centre-style online fraud, and seized 170 devices; on 3 September they arrested six more of the same kind of ring in a Cox's Bazar hotel <sup>[N256](#n256)</sup>. Police said those arrested, citizens of China, Pakistan, Laos, Nepal, Vietnam and Taiwan, were trying to set up scam centres in Bangladesh <sup>[N256](#n256)</sup>. Md Ahsan Habib Palash, Additional Inspector General of Police (Crime and Operations), said in November 2026 that a foreign ring that had taken Bangladeshis to Cambodia and built scam centres there was now trying, with their help, to set them up in Bangladesh <sup>[N256](#n256)</sup>.
- **Victims turned recruiters:** many who were first victims later join the same ring and traffic and cheat others, a CID additional inspector general said in July 2026 <sup>[N244](#n244)</sup>.

<a id="cases-2"></a>

### 11.4 Cases

- Told July 2026: Mannan Soyal, back from three years in Saudi Arabia, was sent to Cambodia by a relative from his area for BDT 5 lakh on the promise of a restaurant job, and left on 5 December 2025 after training <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>. A few days after arriving he was taken to a Chinese-run place with thousands of people at computers and told to call himself "Maria", build relationships with people on Instagram and get money from them <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>. When he objected, his passport was already with them, and he was told to pay the money back if he wanted it <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>. He was beaten and locked in a small room for missing his target, and later freed in a police raid <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>.
- Told Aug 2026: Tofayel Ahmed came to Dhaka to go to Saudi Arabia, but at a friend's urging went to Cambodia through two brokers, travelling with a BMET smart card <sup>[N45](#n45)</sup>. He was held about one month and 27 days before he escaped; he was never given the one-year visa and work permit, and became illegal when his one-month single-entry visa ran out <sup>[N45](#n45)</sup>.
- Reported Oct 2026: two young men had about BDT 6 lakh each taken, their passports taken on arrival, and were sold to a scam centre for USD 5,000 <sup>[N245](#n245)</sup>. The CID said they were tortured if they refused the fraud work or made even a small mistake, and had no way to leave <sup>[N245](#n245)</sup>. Their families paid a further USD 5,000 ransom, the traffickers kept control of them after release, and money was collected once more for the tickets home <sup>[N245](#n245)</sup>. A case was filed at Paltan police station on 26 October 2026 naming Md Shaon Molla (23) and Md Sakib Sarkar (26) of Pubail, Gazipur, and the CID arrested both the next day <sup>[N245](#n245)</sup>.

<a id="the-numbers-7"></a>

### 11.5 The numbers

- **Clearances:** BMET cleared 15,921 workers for Cambodia in 18 months, and BRAC says more than 1,000 of them were trapped in compounds <sup>[N257](#n257)</sup>; Prothom Alo reported more than 15,000 cleared in the year and a half to July 2026 <sup>[N72](#n72)</sup>. In 2025 alone 12,263 took clearance for Cambodia, the eighth-largest destination that year, against 4,727 for Russia <sup>[N99](#n99), [N245](#n245)</sup>.
- **Returns:** 583 trafficking victims came home from Cambodia in June 2026, including 221 in four days, and another 109 on 1 July <sup>[N19](#n19), [N158](#n158)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported 646 back in June after Cambodian raids <sup>[N99](#n99), [N245](#n245)</sup>. About 1,800 had come home as victims of cyber fraud in 2026 by June, according to BRAC <sup>[N244](#n244), [N159](#n159)</sup>. By April 2026 about 50 people rescued from Myawaddy, Myanmar, had been repatriated through the Embassy in Bangkok <sup>[N250](#n250)</sup>.
- **What returnees went through:** BRAC found 88% of returnees from Cambodia were held captive with no freedom of movement, 38% suffered serious mental trauma and 27% physical torture <sup>[N72](#n72), [N159](#n159)</sup>. One report of BRAC's figures gives 86% who had their passports and papers taken, another 87% who had forged papers <sup>[N72](#n72), [N159](#n159)</sup>.
- **Laos:** 7,532 Bangladeshis went to Laos in the 21 months before October 2026 <sup>[N258](#n258)</sup>. About 3,000 took clearance for Laos in the first eight months of 2026 (13th destination), against 3,835 in 2025 (16th), mainly for construction and manufacturing, BMET sources said <sup>[N259](#n259)</sup>.
- **Region:** IOM helped more than 3,500 victims of forced criminality in Southeast Asia in 2022 to 2025, Bangladesh among the largest origin countries <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.
- **Gaps in the data:** Bangladesh has no reliable national data on how many people are trafficked to scam centres, come home or get justice, according to UNODC (the UN Office on Drugs and Crime) in July 2026 <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - How many people came home from Cambodia in June 2026: 583 or 646?
> - In BRAC's returnee survey, is the 86-87% figure for people whose passports were taken, or for people carrying forged papers?

<a id="the-law-7"></a>

### 11.6 The law

- **Bangladesh:** the 2026 Act counts forcing a person into crimes such as online scamming as exploitation under the trafficking offence <sup>[3](#o3), [N261](#n261)</sup>. Since July 2026 workers for Cambodia, Laos and Brazil need a proper work visa and full checks before BMET clearance <sup>[N19](#n19)</sup>. Most published lists of the 12 countries needing Ministry approval since 4 November 2026 include Cambodia (see [The Balkans and Eastern Europe](#the-balkans-and-eastern-europe)) <sup>[N150](#n150), [N152](#n152), [N149](#n149)</sup>. A Home Ministry joint secretary said in July 2026 that cooperation with Cambodia and others against scam centres has been strengthened, and that quick legal action is taken against traffickers even if they are government officials <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>.
- **Cambodia:** running a scam centre with violence or detention carries 10 to 20 years in prison <sup>[N262](#n262)</sup>. The 2026 US Trafficking in Persons report says some senior Cambodian officials and police profit from and protect scam centres <sup>[N263](#n263)</sup>. Cambodian law enforcement shut scam centres under international pressure in mid-2026, after which 583 workers came home in June <sup>[N72](#n72)</sup>.
- **Laos:** recruitment, work permits and entry visas for Bangladeshi workers are suspended from 1 September to 31 December 2026 <sup>[N264](#n264)</sup>. The Ministry of Labour and Social Welfare's directive of 9 September 2026 cites illegal entry, trafficking, unauthorised labour suppliers and employers who evade responsibility, without saying why Bangladeshis were singled out <sup>[N259](#n259)</sup>. No person, company, employer or labour supplier may on its own let Bangladeshis enter or work in Laos, and the LA-B2 labour visa stays closed to them until the end of 2026, except for large government-approved priority projects <sup>[N259](#n259)</sup>. A worker found working outside his approved workplace is fined 1 million kip (USD 44) each time and sent home within 30 days at the cost of the employer or whoever broke the rule; employers pay 2.5 million kip (USD 110) per worker for hiring without approval, tripled for a repeat <sup>[N259](#n259)</sup>. Asked in September 2026, the Expatriates' Welfare Minister said nothing was officially known and the matter would be looked into <sup>[N259](#n259)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-get-help-6"></a>

### 11.7 Where to get help

- **Bangladesh Embassy, Bangkok** (covers Cambodia; there is no embassy in Phnom Penh): 47/8 Ekamai Soi 30, Sukhumvit 63, Bangkok 10110; +66 (0)2 390 5107 to 8; welfare hotline +66-818-70-84-43 <sup>[34](#o34), [N265](#n265)</sup>. Tofayel Ahmed applied to return through the embassy on Facebook after police raids began and came home on 14 June once it was approved <sup>[N45](#n45)</sup>.
- **Cambodian police anti-trafficking hotline:** 1288 <sup>[36 (2022)](#o36)</sup>.
- **BRAC:** families who contacted BRAC got 18 men out of a Myanmar compound in March 2025, with the Foreign Ministry, the CID and the Embassy in Thailand <sup>[N242](#n242)</sup>. Mannan Soyal had no passport, and BRAC arranged a travel pass and a ticket home <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>. Two young men stuck in a Cambodian hotel for about two months after release reached the BRAC Migration Centre online and came home on 3 September 2026 <sup>[N245](#n245)</sup>.
- **Through the Wage Earners' Welfare Board:** after an application from Anirban Survivor Voice Foundation, the Board's expatriate welfare branch wrote to the labour wing of the Bangkok embassy asking it to free the 30 detained in Cambodia and send them home quickly <sup>[N254](#n254)</sup>. For the eight held in a Mae Sot immigration safe home in Thailand, the Foundation applied to the Board on 24 August 2026 on complaints from victims and families, and the Board's director instructed the embassy's First Secretary (Labour) to free them and send them home <sup>[N251](#n251)</sup>. Three were due home on 22 October 2026, while the other five were still in the legal process <sup>[N251](#n251)</sup>.
- **After return:** returnees need to be treated as survivors, not criminals, with regular follow-up, rehabilitation and mental health care so they are not drawn back into the rings, the Foundation's president said in July 2026 <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>.

<a id="kyrgyzstan-and-central-asia-as-a-stepping-stone"></a>

## 12 Kyrgyzstan and Central Asia as a stepping stone

<a id="how-it-is-sold-9"></a>

### 12.1 How it is sold

The offer is Europe: a Shariatpur man paid Tk 4.8 lakh in 2025 and a Sylhet man Tk 6 lakh in 2023 to be taken to Italy or Poland <sup>[N144](#n144), [N266 (2023)](#n266)</sup>. CID says Kyrgyzstan became a trafficking route because it is seen as close to Europe <sup>[N165 (2024)](#n165)</sup>. Others are sold factory jobs and sent on 59-day tourist visas <sup>[N165 (2024)](#n165)</sup>.

<a id="the-path-7"></a>

### 12.2 The path

1. <a id="the-path-7-item-1"></a>**Dhaka to Dubai** on a visit visa <sup>[N165 (2024)](#n165)</sup>.
2. <a id="the-path-7-item-2"></a>**Dubai to Bishkek**, Kyrgyzstan's capital, on a tourist visa <sup>[N165 (2024)](#n165), [N144](#n144)</sup>.
3. <a id="the-path-7-item-3"></a>**The promised next step** (a Ukraine visa, or Italy "after some work") never comes; the Sylhet broker left for Dubai and disappeared <sup>[N143 (2023)](#n143)</sup>.
4. <a id="the-path-7-item-4"></a>**Overland**, some cross into Kazakhstan away from checkpoints to look for work, and have been caught as far as the Russian border <sup>[N267 (2024)](#n267)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-8"></a>

### 12.3 What happens

- **Abandoned:** about 40 Bangladeshis were found abandoned at one Bishkek address in 2024, and had to go to Uzbekistan and back to get visas <sup>[N268 (2024)](#n268)</sup>.
- **Prison and bans:** Bangladeshis caught crossing Kazakhstan to the Russian border in 2024 got six months in prison, expulsion and a five-year ban <sup>[N267 (2024)](#n267)</sup>.
- **Fines and deportation:** breaking the purpose of entry, such as working on a tourist visa, is fined 10,000 som (Kyrgyz currency) in Kyrgyzstan, and in September 2025, 180 Bangladeshis were sent back <sup>[N269](#n269), [N270](#n270)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-8"></a>

### 12.4 The law

- **Kyrgyzstan:** a foreigner who works without a permit is to be expelled <sup>[N271](#n271)</sup>. Its security chief says foreigners come as tourists and students and work illegally <sup>[N272](#n272)</sup>.
- **Kazakhstan:** prosecutors detained three organisers who smuggled 20 South Asians from Kyrgyzstan in November 2023 <sup>[N273 (2023)](#n273)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-get-help-7"></a>

### 12.5 Where to get help

Bangladesh has no embassy in Kyrgyzstan; the Embassy in Tashkent, Uzbekistan, covers it, with a 24-hour emergency number +998930009780 <sup>[N274 (2024)](#n274), [N275 (2024)](#n275)</sup>. BRAC asks stranded migrants to contact its Migration Welfare Centre <sup>[N276](#n276)</sup>.

<a id="russia-recruited-into-the-war"></a>

## 13 Russia: recruited into the war

<a id="how-it-is-sold-10"></a>

### 13.1 How it is sold

**The jobs offered:**

- construction or welding, through a recruiting agency, at about 75,000 roubles a month <sup>[N277](#n277), [N166](#n166)</sup>; masonry, for two Jamalpur men who went through a broker in April 2026 <sup>[N278](#n278)</sup>;
- work in Russia and in European countries, offered by a Bangladeshi agency, the Foreign Ministry spokesperson said in February 2025 <sup>[N279](#n279)</sup>;
- a cleaning job: in an investigation by AP (the Associated Press, a US news agency) reported in January 2026, Maksudur Rahman said a labour broker persuaded him to go to Russia as a janitor <sup>[N280](#n280)</sup>;
- high-paid work in oil, construction or logistics, according to "Promises Written in Blood", a November 2025 report by BRAC's Migration Programme based on survivors' accounts, family testimony and document checks <sup>[N281](#n281)</sup>;
- work as a cook or cleaner, according to an Associated Press (AP) investigation summarised in a Prothom Alo editorial of 4 February 2026 <sup>[N282](#n282)</sup>; Jahangir Alam of Lakshmipur was promised work as a cook in a cantonment in 2025 <sup>[N72](#n72)</sup>;
- security guard, driver, mechanic, electrician, construction worker, welder, repair or transport worker or technician, and sometimes IT, medicine, sport or agriculture, offered through social media adverts or recruiting agents and firms, according to the UN's Independent International Commission of Inquiry on Ukraine; some recruiters said the work was under the military but civilian <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>. The commission describes a professional athlete told he could sign a contract after arriving if he liked the work, who was handed to the military on arrival, and a cook who did not want to be a soldier but ended up on the front line <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>.

**The promises:** the UN commission says the offers spoke of high pay, Russian citizenship, comfortable housing, a pension after leaving the job and other benefits, and some recruits were shown photos or a catalogue of the housing <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>. Survivors say brokers repeatedly promised it was not military work <sup>[N166](#n166)</sup>. The commission describes a group of 30 given a letter of assurance that they would not be sent to the battlefield; by the time of its report 17 had been killed on the front line, 5 were missing and 1 had returned wounded; the report does not name the group's country <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>. One family was offered medical school in Moscow for their son if he first served four months in the war <sup>[N277](#n277)</sup>.

**When Europe falls through:** a young man fighting for Russia told Prothom Alo in April 2025 that a broker who had taken his passport for a North Macedonia work visa later said that visa would not happen and offered instead a Russian work visa at a good company paying 40,000 to 45,000 roubles (Tk 60,000 to 70,000) a month, for Tk 8 lakh <sup>[N284](#n284)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported in June 2025 that traffickers are taking people bound for Europe and forcing them to join the Russian forces <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>. Suruj Kazi (35) of Madaripur, according to his family, turned to an agency for Russia after failing to get a European visa through a Bangladeshi broker despite many attempts <sup>[N286](#n286)</sup>.

**On Facebook:** Shariful Hasan of BRAC said in November 2025 that a Bangladeshi using the name MD Sohel was campaigning on Facebook about the benefits of joining the Russian army and fighting, and that many people were being newly lured to Russia to fight because of it <sup>[N281](#n281)</sup>.

**Going to fight on purpose:** Yasin Sheikh (22) went to Russia on 4 September 2024 through a firm in Uttara with BDT 15 lakh his brother raised from selling land and borrowing, said in a Facebook video that he worked three months and then joined the army voluntarily online, and signed on as a contract fighter on 22 December 2024 <sup>[N287](#n287)</sup>; he was killed on 27 March 2025 <sup>[N281](#n281)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported in October 2026 that travel to Russia had not decreased, and that because six months at the war brings a chance of Russian citizenship and pay of Tk 3 to 4 lakh a month, many go to the war of their own accord <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. Bangladesh's ambassador to Russia, Md Nazrul Islam, said in October 2026 that more than 90% of the workers go to the war of their own accord, and that Russia says the latest 30 also went voluntarily and holds written proof of it <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.

**The cost:** families paid about Tk 9.5 to 10 lakh, about USD 8,000 <sup>[N166](#n166), [N277](#n277), [N5](#n5)</sup>. The police's Special Response Battalion (SRB) said about BDT 10 lakh on average was taken from each of the 30 men who flew on 7 May 2026 <sup>[N289](#n289)</sup>. BRAC's November 2025 report puts it at Tk 7 to 8 lakh <sup>[N281](#n281)</sup>. The UN commission found that some recruits paid in advance and others were told the money would be deducted from their pay later <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>.

<a id="who-sends-them"></a>

### 13.2 Who sends them

- **Agencies in Bangladesh:** a Prothom Alo investigation found that clearances for Russia were taken in the names of 93 recruiting agencies over four years, that only 17 took clearance for more than a hundred workers each, and that Bonna Bijoy Overseas alone took the most, about 2,500 workers or 27% <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported in October 2026 that Bonna Bijoy Overseas is accused of trafficking in the name of sending workers to Algeria, has a human trafficking case against it, faces allegations over Russia too, and has had its server locked so it can take no new clearances; its managing director, Barun Debnath, did not answer the paper's call or text message <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. BOESL, the government's recruiting agency, took the second-highest number of clearances, 771, and a responsible BOESL official told Prothom Alo that one of its workers became involved in the war (see When the visa, the agency and the clearance are all real) <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. Shariful Hasan, head of BRAC's Migration Programme, said in October 2026 that the agencies accused of trafficking are sending the most workers to Russia <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.
- **The ring:** the February 2026 Prothom Alo editorial, citing the AP investigation, said a Bangladeshi living in Moscow is the trafficking ring's main organiser, that investigators suspect it has links with the Russian government, and named "SP Global" as one of the firms that ran such trafficking openly <sup>[N282](#n282)</sup>. BRAC's report says international facilitators as well as local brokers are involved <sup>[N281](#n281)</sup>.
- **A Banani travel agency and a ringleader:** the CID (the Criminal Investigation Department of the police) arrested a woman named in its statements both as Fabiha Jerin and as Tamanna, a partner in Dream Home Travels and Tours Ltd of Banani, Dhaka, at Dhaka airport on 5 February 2025 as she was about to flee to Nepal, in a case filed at Banani police station by the wife of a wounded victim; the CID said she confessed and that her brother Tuhin, in Russia, had encouraged people to go to the war <sup>[N290](#n290), [N291](#n291)</sup>. In June 2025 the CID arrested the alleged ringleader, Muhammad Alamgir Hossain (40) of Lohagara, Chattogram, at Chattogram's Shah Amanat airport; it said Alamgir, who went to Russia on a student visa in 2008 and became a citizen by marriage, had taken 50 people to Russia on three visa categories <sup>[N291](#n291)</sup>.
- **The 7 May 2026 group:** the SRB said on 14 October 2026 that the 30 men went through three firms, Jabale Noor, RS International and TS Group, that a man named Igor in Russia and a woman named Julia in Kyrgyzstan were involved alongside the Bangladeshi ring, and that it was trying to arrest another member, Mostafizur <sup>[N289](#n289)</sup>.
- **Brokers inside Russia:** Bangladeshi brokers already in Russia recruit men who went for other work. Mohammad Akram Hossain (25) of Ashuganj, Brahmanbaria, learned welding at a training centre in Amtali, Palash, Narsingdi, went to Russia about eight months before April 2025 for Tk 6.5 lakh borrowed, and worked there as a welder sending home Tk 80,000 to 1 lakh a month; according to his family, two and a half months before his death in April 2025 he fell into a broker's hands in Russia and joined the army as a contract fighter <sup>[N292](#n292)</sup>. Two Jamalpur men sent for masonry in April 2026 had to join the army after falling into a Bangladeshi broker's hands, and Suruj Kazi, who went for ordinary work, joined after the same <sup>[N278](#n278), [N286](#n286)</sup>. Mohammad Sagar of Chandina, Cumilla, working at a bakery, was promised a job in the Russian army and a one-time BDT 30 lakh by a Bangladeshi he met there, and joined the war in May 2026 <sup>[N23](#n23)</sup>.
- **Agents abroad:** the UN commission says recruiting agents in the various countries were local, regional or Russian nationals operating as travel or tourism businesses; some held local licences, some presented themselves as legal or government-approved, and in many cases visas issued by the Russian embassy were handed over through them <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>.

<a id="the-path-8"></a>

### 13.3 The path

1. <a id="the-path-8-item-1"></a>**A fast departure.** The UN commission found many recruits were pressed to travel within a few days of accepting, with agents arranging the visa, air ticket, housing and the rest of the journey; visas were business visas, short-term tourist visas or none, varying by country <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>. It obtained copies of business visas of recruited Bangladeshis that mention an invitation from a Russian civilian company <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>.
2. <a id="the-path-8-item-2"></a>**Dhaka to Dubai, then Moscow**, or through other third countries <sup>[N166](#n166)</sup>. Tasneem Siddiqui, executive director of RMMRU (the Refugee and Migratory Movements Research Unit), said in November 2025 that many are first taken to Saudi Arabia on tourist or Umrah visas and later made to sign a contract and forced to the battlefield <sup>[N281](#n281)</sup>. The CID said in June 2025 that a ring lured 10 people with jobs as cleaners or cooks in a chocolate factory in Russia at Tk 2-2.5 lakh a month, sent them first to Saudi Arabia on Umrah visas, had them perform Umrah, then took them to Russia and sold them to a man called Sultan, who handed them to the Russian army <sup>[N291](#n291)</sup>. Testimony and documents the UN commission obtained name Bahrain, Brazil, Ethiopia, France, Kenya, Mexico, Morocco, Oman, Panama, Spain, Turkey and the UAE as transit countries; agents sometimes travelled with the recruits or met them there, and some recruits were told to go by road to a neighbouring country and fly from there so as not to be caught at the airport <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>.
3. <a id="the-path-8-item-3"></a>**At Moscow airport**, brokers take more cash, in one case about USD 1,000 per person <sup>[N166](#n166)</sup>. The UN commission found many recruits had no freedom of movement from the airport on: they were kept in temporary accommodation and handed to the armed forces <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>.
4. <a id="the-path-8-item-4"></a>**Passports and phones taken**, and the men are handed to the military <sup>[N5](#n5), [N14](#n14)</sup>.
5. <a id="the-path-8-item-5"></a>**Russian-language contracts** they can't read <sup>[N166](#n166)</sup>. According to the UN commission, within hours or days of arriving recruits were made to sign a one-year military service contract with Russia's Defence Ministry, and could not use translation because their phones were not in their hands <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>. Those who refused were pressured, threatened or tortured; one recruit said four people were shot dead in front of him and he was told he would be next, after which he signed <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>.

Some go legally on six-month work visas; when the visa ends they are offered a military contract to renew it <sup>[N277](#n277)</sup>. The young man who spoke to Prothom Alo in April 2025 described how this happens:

1. <a id="russia-recruited-into-the-war-item-1"></a>**The job.** He was sent for welding but worked as an electrician for the Chinese company Sinopec in Blagoveshchensk, near the Chinese border; pay became irregular, and at the start of February 2025 the company handed him his passport and put him out with about 20 others <sup>[N284](#n284)</sup>.
2. <a id="russia-recruited-into-the-war-item-2"></a>**The airport.** After 20 days living at and around a Moscow airport, a man there told them through Google Translate that their visas were still valid and offered them work <sup>[N284](#n284)</sup>.
3. <a id="russia-recruited-into-the-war-item-3"></a>**The contract.** At a hotel he took their passports and papers, and three or four Russians had them sign a contract they could not read, calling it a work contract, without letting them photograph and translate it <sup>[N284](#n284)</sup>.
4. <a id="russia-recruited-into-the-war-item-4"></a>**The sale.** Promised meat-cutting work, they were taken to a town far from Moscow to clear snow, then to a forest to cut trees, where weapons arrived with soldiers in civilian clothes and the people who had brought them slipped away <sup>[N284](#n284)</sup>. When the five begged the commander to let them go, he asked why they had signed the contract and said nothing could be done now <sup>[N284](#n284)</sup>. Workers laid off from Russian jobs have also been recruited there (see "Recruited while abroad or just back" in the Dalal scams doc) <sup>[N281](#n281)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-9"></a>

### 13.4 What happens

- **Training:** the UN commission found initial training lasted from 20 minutes to a few days, in some cases up to a month <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>. Jahangir Alam of Lakshmipur said he was handed a weapon without training and then taught to use it for one day, that 16 men were together in his war camp, and that he lived in fear after four of them died <sup>[N72](#n72)</sup>. Khadija Akter said in February 2025 that her husband, Jafar Sardar of Jashore, was kept in a barracks, given an army haircut and war training, and that his last message said "bring me home at any cost" <sup>[N293](#n293)</sup>. The young man who spoke to Prothom Alo in April 2025 said he was trained for five days from 20 March in loading and firing guns and sent towards Ukraine on the sixth day, and that on a mission on 7 or 8 April, 12 went out and 6 came back <sup>[N284](#n284)</sup>.
- **Work at the front:** the UN commission says recruits were made to dig trenches, guard areas, deliver food and water and collect bodies, their main tasks being to carry out assaults and advance <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>. Ali Hasan Sohel of the group of 30 said men who refused to fight were locked up, beaten and punished, and that they were handed a weapon and told they need not fight, only move forward, back, left and right, which he called bait and a death trap because whoever went was hit by Ukrainian drones <sup>[N294](#n294)</sup>. In the AP investigation, returned workers said they were made to collect bodies and move the wounded, those who refused were tortured, and a Russian commander told one victim he had in fact been "sold" <sup>[N282](#n282)</sup>. The SRB, the detective branch and others told Prothom Alo that the men sent from Bangladesh have neither the physical fitness, the training nor the tactics for war, and that when the enemy attacks them with drones Russian soldiers identify the enemy's position <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.
- **Death:** 30 men flew out on 7 May 2026 <sup>[N277](#n277)</sup>. In October 2026 the commander of the police's Special Response Battalion said 17 who arrived that May were killed on their first day of deployment after minimal training, 20 Bangladeshis were confirmed dead, and they had been used as bait to locate enemy positions <sup>[N5](#n5)</sup>. On 9 July 2026 the Minister had told Parliament that four of the 30 had died <sup>[N4](#n4)</sup>. A joint investigation by Fortify Rights and Truth Hounds, two rights groups, counted at least 104 Bangladeshis recruited and at least 34 killed <sup>[N277](#n277)</sup>. The news agency Agence France-Presse (AFP) reported that Mohammad Yasin Sheikh, 22, was killed on 27 March 2025 fighting for Russian forces <sup>[N281](#n281)</sup>. According to Jafar Sardar's family, the broker took 10 Bangladeshis to Russia together; one, Akram of Narsingdi, escaped and came home, and the other nine were forced to the battlefield, where one was killed and one was shot <sup>[N293](#n293)</sup>. Other deaths reported by families:
  - Mohammad Akram Hossain of Ashuganj, killed in a Ukrainian attack, according to a call his father got from Russia on 17 April 2025; his father said Akram had told him soldiers beat him when he did not obey, and that Tk 4 lakh had been paid into his Russian bank account; a local union parishad chairman, citing the family, said Russia had given him citizenship <sup>[N292](#n292)</sup>;
  - Suruj Kazi (35) of Madaripur, killed by a mine in May 2026; his death was at first kept hidden and two Bangladeshis in Russia told the family by phone <sup>[N286](#n286)</sup>;
  - Mafol Mia (24) and Md Arif Mia (30) of Madarganj, Jamalpur, killed in drone attacks, confirmed on 15 June 2026 through a Bangladeshi in Russia; Arif had left a job in the Ansar (a government auxiliary force) to go, and Mafol's sister said he was given no work on arrival and phoned once to say he had been given to the army and sent to war <sup>[N278](#n278)</sup>;
  - Abdur Rahim (30) of Fulbaria, Mymensingh, seven years a welder in Singapore, who went to Russia on 7 December 2024 for about BDT 5 lakh as his family believed for welding work; three months before his death he took BDT 3 lakh more from them for what he called a new power-plant job paying BDT 2 lakh a month, and sent BDT 1 lakh 40 thousand the month before; he was in fact in army training, joined on 1 May 2026 and was killed by a drone the next day <sup>[N295](#n295)</sup>. His mother said a dalal took him from welding into the army, and the family learned of his death through Limon Datta of Narsingdi, a wounded friend from the same camp <sup>[N295](#n295)</sup>;
  - Mohammad Sagar of Chandina, Cumilla, who joined in May 2026; the Chandina Upazila Nirbahi Officer said on 19 October 2026 that a message from a friend of Sagar's was the only information that he had been killed; Sagar had sent BDT 3 lakh to his uncle's bank account before going to the war, and could not be reached after <sup>[N23](#n23)</sup>;
  - Al Mamun of Nandail, Mymensingh, who went to Russia to ease his family's poverty; Prothom Alo reported on 13 September 2026 that his family had news he was killed in a drone attack after falling into a broker ring's hands <sup>[N296](#n296)</sup>.
- **Abuse:** survivors were sent to fight with little or no training, beaten, unpaid, kept from leaving and threatened when they tried to escape <sup>[N166](#n166)</sup>. Maksudur Rahman told AP they were threatened with 10 years in prison and beaten if they refused to work; he escaped and came home after seven months, and victims told AP they had seen hundreds of Bangladeshis with the Russian forces in Ukraine <sup>[N280](#n280)</sup>. The young man who spoke to Prothom Alo said they were not allowed to call their families, that he called hiding by or in the toilet and was beaten if seen, and that in camp they got dry bread and pasta <sup>[N284](#n284)</sup>. Recruits told the UN commission of days without food and water, beatings, detention and death threats <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>.
- **Pay:** the UN commission found recruits were taken to open bank accounts, but many were not paid and some got little; money was withdrawn from some accounts without permission, some salaries were withheld or accounts closed, and in some cases recruiting agents or military personnel took the bank cards <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>.
- **Missing:** many recruits lost contact with their families after going to the war, the UN commission found <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>. BRAC's November 2025 report records that Ayon Mondal of Bagerhat, who went on a valid visa, said in his last message that he was being taken towards the Ukrainian border and has not been traced since, and that Amit Barua of Cumilla has been missing since a photo showed him in Russian military uniform <sup>[N281](#n281)</sup>. Adnan Hossain, a wounded returnee, said Russia does not publish information on Bangladeshis' deaths and that anyone who does not return from the front line is declared missing <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.
- **Captured:** Prothom Alo reported in July 2026 that two young Bangladeshis drawn into the war through brokers surrendered to Ukrainian troops and ended up in a Ukrainian prison <sup>[N297](#n297)</sup>. Rimel Mia (22) of Sundarganj, Gaibandha, went in March 2025 through a broker in Gaibandha town for a construction job, on money from selling his father's inherited land and a Tk 7 lakh loan at high interest; he sent home Tk 1.3 lakh in the first three months and then contact stopped <sup>[N298](#n298)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported in August 2026 that after losing the job and falling into legal trouble he was forced to join the army, sent to Ukraine after a few days' training and captured, and is held as a prisoner of war; his family learned this only when The Daily Star published an interview with him and two other Bangladeshis <sup>[N298](#n298)</sup>. His father said the debt had grown to Tk 9 lakh with interest and was being paid from another son's earnings in Saudi Arabia <sup>[N298](#n298)</sup>. The UN commission says trafficked men who surrender or are captured are entitled to prisoner-of-war protection until the war ends <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>.
- **Bodies not returned:** many families still cannot get their sons' bodies home <sup>[N277](#n277)</sup>. Adnan Hossain said Russia does not bring back the bodies, and those Prothom Alo spoke to said the bodies of foreigners killed are left on the field <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. The ambassador said the bodies cannot be sent home because the Russian authorities are not handing them over <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.

<a id="the-group-of-30"></a>

### 13.5 The group of 30

The SRB names three firms for this group, Jabale Noor, RS International and TS Group (see [Who sends them](#who-sends-them)) <sup>[N289](#n289)</sup>. The 30 men who flew from Dhaka on 7 May 2026 went through Jabale Noor International of Khilkhet and RS International of Ashulia, through brokers, according to 28 letters their families submitted to the Expatriates' Welfare Ministry in July 2026; the letters say the men were sold to the Russian army with the lure of high pay (see When the visa, the agency and the clearance are all real) <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. Prothom Alo said nearly all the families are low-income <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>.

**What they paid:**

- Rajon Hossain (21) of Jhenaidah paid a broker Tk 10 lakh for a high-paid job <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.
- Pabitra Chandra (30), a carpenter from a very poor family in Gopalpur, Tangail, went with Tk 1.3 lakh from selling his wife's jewellery and Tk 9.5 lakh borrowed from an NGO and local moneylenders <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>.
- Robin Mir, an autorickshaw driver from Siddhirganj, Narayanganj, sold his village land and cattle and borrowed, and went with three other men from Narayanganj <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>.
- Md Palash Sheikh's family spent Tk 10 lakh <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.
- Md Arman Ali (30) of Jamalpur Sadar, who had worked about four years in Iraq, borrowed about Tk 10 lakh and went through an agency on a promise of a company or construction job <sup>[N300](#n300)</sup>; in a video he said the agency's people had promised all 30 jobs at a company or construction site <sup>[N300](#n300)</sup>.

**What happened, in order:**

1. <a id="the-group-of-30-item-1"></a>**The first days.** Robin Mir's nephew, Rahat Chowdhury, said his uncle was given good food and shown around for three or four days, and then there was no news for five or six days <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>. Pabitra Chandra told his brother-in-law, Tapan Chandra Sutradhar, that he and several others were kept in a hotel and would be given work once the papers were sorted out; contact then stopped for some time <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>. Rajon Hossain got no job and, after four days in a hotel, was sent to an army camp after his signature was taken on a paper <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. Arman Ali said in his video that within two days of arriving they were handed to two Russian citizens and then sold for the war <sup>[N300](#n300)</sup>.
2. <a id="the-group-of-30-item-2"></a>**15 to 18 May.** On 15 May Robin Mir said they were being taken to work, and on 17 May he was seen in an army camp in a video <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>. On 18 May a worker's family phoned the Bangladesh Embassy in Russia to say that 30 people who had come through Jabale Noor and RS International were being sent to the battlefield; according to the embassy's 25 May letter, it was writing to Russia's Foreign Ministry asking that the workers be returned to work under their appointment letters instead <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.
3. <a id="the-group-of-30-item-3"></a>**Training.** After 21 days of training the men were split into groups of 16, 8 and 6 and sent to the war <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. Arman Ali said in June 2026 that they got three to four days' training, after which 14 of the 30 went to one camp and 16, including him, to another, and five or six of them were sent to the front line <sup>[N300](#n300)</sup>. Ali Hasan Sohel, who was in the group of 16, said that after only five days of training five of them were sent to the battlefield on the sixth day, and that of his 16, 14 were missing and 2 in hospital by October 2026 <sup>[N294](#n294)</sup>.
4. <a id="the-group-of-30-item-4"></a>**26 May.** Arman Ali phoned his family to say he had been sold to the Russian army; they could not reach him afterwards <sup>[N300](#n300)</sup>.
5. <a id="the-group-of-30-item-5"></a>**29 May.** Pabitra Chandra phoned to say "we are being taken to the war by force; the chance of dying there is 95 per cent" <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>, and afterwards asked the Bangladesh embassy to arrange for him and the others to be brought home <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>. Robin Mir phoned in tears to say they had been taken through deep forest on the promise of work and sold <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>.
6. <a id="the-group-of-30-item-6"></a>**June.** Two wounded returnees and six families said the men pleaded with their families before being sent to war, that the agency promised to take responsibility when the families contacted it, and that from the first week of June the men began to go missing; the families got news of deaths but no proof <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. Many died around 12 and 13 June <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. At its 14 October 2026 press conference the SRB said 17 of the 30 died in a drone attack on their first day on the battlefield and three more died later <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>. Rajon Hossain said he was sent to the front line with five other Bangladeshis, four were killed in a drone attack, and he was wounded <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. In a video reported on 21 June 2026, Arman Ali said 12 of his group of 16 had died and the four left were wounded by drones, two of them still in hospital; he described mines and grenades in the ground and drones overhead, said they were directed by drone and used "like fish bait", and said anyone who refused the front line was beaten and locked in an underground bunker <sup>[N300](#n300)</sup>. The families' October 2026 list of the dead includes a Md Arman Ali <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>. Abdul Majid of Lalmonirhat said his son Abdullah Al Mamun phoned his younger brother the day before being sent to fight and was not heard from again; a man who went with him, Al Amin, later said Mamun had died <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.

**Where they were in October 2026:** Prothom Alo reported 20 dead, two wounded and in Russian hospitals (Palash Sheikh and Ali Hasan Sohel), two back in Bangladesh (Rajon Hossain and Adnan Hossain), and six still being made to fight <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>.

- **The dead,** according to the families: Pabitra Chandra, Robin Mir, Sohel, Md Mehedi Hasan, Md Al Mamun, Md Abdullah Al Mamun, Md Nazmul Alam, Md Wasim Akram, Md Arif Hossain, Md Sayed Molla, Tarikul Islam Mintu, Sabbir Hossain, Mofiz, Md Arman Ali, Md Nazrul Islam, Md Aminur Islam, Kazi Rezaul Karim, Main Uddin, Ripon Hossain and Arif Hossain Patwari <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>. Robin Mir's family learned of his death through the three Narayanganj men he went with <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>. Pabitra Chandra's family heard of his death through his companions and, by 21 October 2026, had not had it confirmed by the embassy or any government channel <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>. In Abdullah Al Mamun's village a funeral in absentia was arranged, but because his death was not confirmed the people who came prayed instead of performing the funeral prayer; five months had passed by October 2026 <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.
- **The six still fighting:** Roni Sheikh and Sourav Molla of Gopalganj, Al Amin Islam of Lalmonirhat, Kamal Hossain and Helal Sheikh of Madaripur and Abu Hanif of Kishoreganj, some of whom keep in contact with their families <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>. Sourav's brother-in-law, Taslim Sardar, who talks to him over the messaging app imo, said Sourav was three to four kilometres behind the front line being made to dig earth for bunkers and had twice tried to escape without success <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>. He said Kamal was digging bunkers with Sourav, Roni Sheikh and Al Amin were carrying food, water and weapons on foot from the camp to the front line, and Hanif and Helal were made sometimes to dig bunkers and sometimes to carry food <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>.
- **The wounded:** Md Palash Sheikh was wounded in his first spell at the war, sent back after recovering, and lost a hand; in October 2026 he was in a Moscow hospital, and his cousin Abu Saleh Islam said asking the embassy and the Ministry had brought no help, that he cannot escape because the hospital is under army guard, and that the family has received no money <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.
- **Ali Hasan Sohel** of Goalanda, Rajbari, was in a hospital in Penza, Russia, in October 2026; he said he had contacted the Bangladesh embassy, which said it would try to bring him home once he recovered, but that once recovered he would be sent back to the war <sup>[N294](#n294)</sup>. The embassy had told Palash Sheikh's family the same <sup>[N294](#n294)</sup>. A responsible official of the Embassy in Moscow said in October 2026 that no wounded man in hospital wanting to come home had yet contacted the embassy to be brought back <sup>[N294](#n294)</sup>.
- **Rajon Hossain** said a drone strike broke his hip and paralysed his lower body, and that he crawled about four kilometres on his chest to reach the camp <sup>[N289](#n289)</sup>.
- **Where they came from:** the SRB said men had gone to the war from Gopalganj, Mymensingh, Narayanganj, Jamalpur, Pabna, Chapainawabganj, Faridpur, Rajbari and Gazipur in particular <sup>[N289](#n289)</sup>.
- **The debts:** Tapan Chandra Sutradhar said in October 2026 that lenders stand behind Pabitra Chandra's family for their money when they sit down to eat, and that the family is thinking of selling its remaining 3 decimals of land, which would still not clear the debt <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Is the "Sohel" the families list among the dead a different man from Ali Hasan Sohel of Goalanda, who spoke to Prothom Alo from a hospital in Penza in October 2026 <sup>[N299](#n299), [N294](#n294)</sup>?
> - Was the group held in the hotel for three days, as the families' complaint says <sup>[N301](#n301)</sup>, or four, as Rajon Hossain says <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>?
> - How long were the 30 trained and how were they split: 21 days and groups of 16, 8 and 6 (Prothom Alo, from the two returnees) <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>, three to four days and groups of 14 and 16 (Arman Ali, June 2026) <sup>[N300](#n300)</sup>, or five days (Ali Hasan Sohel, October 2026) <sup>[N294](#n294)</sup>?

<a id="the-numbers-8"></a>

### 13.6 The numbers

- **Clearances:** BMET gave 7,449 clearances for Russia between 1 January 2025 and 27 October 2026 <sup>[N277](#n277)</sup>. BMET data show 37 people took clearance for Russia in 2023, 1,395 in 2024, 4,725 in 2025 and 3,105 in 2026 up to 15 October <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. According to BMET, 4,727 Bangladeshis went to Russia in 2025, which made it the 13th largest market for Bangladeshi workers that year, ahead of Malaysia, South Korea and Lebanon <sup>[N99](#n99), [N245](#n245)</sup>. RMMRU says 4,727 went in 2025 against 993 the year before, and that 2,125 took clearance for Russia in the first six months of 2026 <sup>[N72](#n72)</sup>.
- **Workers in Russia:** according to a Foreign Ministry press release of 9 June 2026, about 10,000 Bangladeshi workers were in Russia, and Dhaka proposed raising this to one lakh within a year, which the Russian side agreed to work on quickly <sup>[N302](#n302)</sup>.
- **Families asking for help:** the Bangladesh Embassy in Moscow told AFP in April 2025 that about a dozen families had contacted it to get their sons back, all alleging the sons were deceived into the Russian army <sup>[N281](#n281)</sup>. By November 2025, 10 families had asked BRAC to bring relatives back from Russia, men who had gone on valid visas to work; BRAC's report says there are more, but the number could not be confirmed <sup>[N281](#n281)</sup>.
- **Deaths:** a Prothom Alo editorial of 4 February 2026 said at least 40 Bangladeshis were believed to have died in the previous few months after being deceived by recruiting agencies into the war <sup>[N282](#n282)</sup>. Foreign Ministry sources said in October 2026 that over four to five months the Russian government had told the embassy of 40 Bangladeshis missing or killed, without separating the two, and had said nothing yet about the latest 20 deaths <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. Prothom Alo had news of at least 54 Bangladeshi deaths in the war over the three years to October 2026 <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. Shariful Hasan of BRAC said in October 2026 that nobody has a count of how many found work, where the rest are or how many have died <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.
- **Worldwide:** a UN commission found men from 29 countries recruited into Russia's forces through deception and false job offers <sup>[N277](#n277)</sup>. Its draft report, published on the website of the UN human rights office (OHCHR) on 26 October 2026, examined recruitment of nationals of 48 countries and found evidence of deception or coercion for 29, Bangladesh among them, but gives no separate figures, names or outcomes for Bangladeshis <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>. Its findings rest on 245 testimonies from recruits detained by Ukraine or back home, their families, lawyers, local authorities, researchers and journalists, plus documents such as invitation letters, visas, air tickets and military contracts; the victims were all men aged 18 to 60, mostly from financially weak and marginal groups <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - BMET's yearly figures (4,725 clearances in 2025 and 3,105 in 2026 to 15 October) add up to more than the 7,449 clearances given for 1 January 2025 to 27 October 2026 <sup>[N288](#n288), [N277](#n277)</sup>: which count is right?
> - For 2024, RMMRU gives 993 Bangladeshis going to Russia and BMET 1,395 clearances <sup>[N72](#n72), [N288](#n288)</sup>: does one count departures and the other clearances?

<a id="legal-jobs-in-russia"></a>

### 13.7 Legal jobs in Russia

- **Through BOESL:** in June 2024 BOESL (Bangladesh Overseas Employment and Services Limited, the government's recruiting agency) advertised 80 jobs for men at a shipbuilding complex in Russia, paying Tk 54,000 for a ship painter, Tk 60,000 for an outfitting assembler and Tk 66,000 for a hull or pipe fitter, for people with at least six months' shipbuilding experience in South Korea, Singapore or elsewhere <sup>[N303 (2024)](#n303)</sup>. Selected workers paid BOESL's Tk 56,350 service charge plus Tk 2,000 for the medical and Tk 10,200 for visa stamping and other fees; nothing is paid before final selection and every fee goes through a bank <sup>[N303 (2024)](#n303)</sup>. The employer paid the air fare both ways, food was the worker's own cost, and the work meant three to five months a year at minus 11 to minus 30 degrees Celsius <sup>[N303 (2024)](#n303)</sup>.
- **Other jobs:** Zakir Hossain of Bhairab was sent on 4 February 2025 for Tk 7 lakh (Tk 4 lakh from selling land, Tk 2 lakh from an NGO loan) to a job at the Amur gas chemical complex paying about Tk 60,000 a month, which after his own costs left little to send home; the family still owed about Tk 1.5 lakh when an explosion at the complex in August 2026 killed him, left another Bangladeshi missing and injured seven <sup>[N304](#n304), [N305](#n305)</sup>. The embassy said the complex is run under China's Sinopec Engineering Group <sup>[N305](#n305)</sup>. Mohammad Sagar went in mid-2025 for BDT 6 lakh through a former shop manager and earned about BDT 70,000 a month at a bakery before he was recruited for the war <sup>[N23](#n23)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-9"></a>

### 13.8 The law

- **Bangladesh:** at a Dhaka briefing on 9 February 2025 the Foreign Ministry spokesperson, Mohammad Rafiqul Alam, said some Bangladeshis had been forced into the war after falling into traffickers' hands, that the Embassy in Moscow had been asked for details, that the relevant ministries had been asked to act against the recruiting and travel agencies involved, and that one member of a ring trafficking people to Russia had been arrested <sup>[N281](#n281)</sup>. He said the airports had been asked to take special caution over Bangladeshis going to Russia on travel visas and those with a valid Russian travel visa in their passports <sup>[N281](#n281)</sup>. In June 2026 the government cancelled the licences of three agencies accused of sending the 30 men, and forfeited their deposits <sup>[N306](#n306)</sup>; on 9 July 2026 the Expatriates' Welfare Minister told Parliament the three licences had been suspended <sup>[N4](#n4)</sup>, and later that month told Prothom Alo they had been cancelled <sup>[N72](#n72)</sup>. In October 2026 police arrested two men accused of the trafficking <sup>[N5](#n5)</sup>. The SRB said Mizanur Rahman, founder of Jabale Noor International and described as one of the ring's leaders, and Abu Taher alias Lentu were arrested on 13 October 2026 over the sending of the 30 men <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. Sources in the police's Criminal Investigation Department (CID) said in October 2026 that at least six cases had been filed over trafficking to Russia <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. Most published lists of the 12 countries needing Ministry approval since 4 November 2026 include Russia <sup>[N150](#n150), [N151](#n151), [N149](#n149)</sup>. On 9 November 2026 the member of parliament Rehana Akter demanded in Parliament the punishment of traffickers, compensation for victims' families and the quick return of Bangladeshis on the battlefield in Russia <sup>[N35](#n35)</sup>.
- **What the Minister says is being done:** Expatriates' Welfare Minister Ariful Haque Chowdhury said in October 2026 that nobody is given clearance without verification and attestation of confirmed job information, that all embassies have been told to keep watch through the expatriate community, that extra caution is being taken over Russia with appointment letters checked much more thoroughly, and that action has begun against the agencies involved <sup>[N288](#n288), [N283](#n283)</sup>. Selim Reza, coordinator of North South University's Centre for Migration Studies, said in October 2026 that the government went on giving clearance for Russia even after learning men were being taken to the war by force, and that demand in Russia's labour market had not been properly assessed <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>.
- **International:** the UN commission concluded that the Russian authorities committed human trafficking together with recruiting agents; that trafficked men, having become members of the Russian armed forces by signing the contract, cannot be called mercenaries; and that if they surrender or are captured they are entitled to prisoner-of-war protection until the war ends <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>. It recommended that Russia stop recruiting foreigners through deception or coercion, discharge and assist the trafficked men in its forces, and prosecute those involved; according to international media citing Russia's UN mission, Russia does not recognise the commission's mandate and says the report rests on false information <sup>[N283](#n283)</sup>.

<a id="bodies-and-compensation"></a>

### 13.9 Bodies and compensation

- **From Russia:** Foreign Ministry sources said in October 2026 that compensation can be claimed if someone dies in the war or is missing for more than six months, but that it means a complicated process, including establishing who the heir is and the heir going to Russia and staying at least six months, and that the bodies cannot be recovered <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.
- **From Bangladesh:** the Wage Earners' Welfare Board says it pays to bring home the body of a worker who went with BMET clearance or registered as a member with the embassy abroad, on the family's application, and that a family with neither can still apply, in which case the Board sends a proposal to the Expatriates' Welfare Ministry, which pays from a yearly allocation <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. It gives Tk 35,000 in cash for the burial of each body brought home and then a Tk 3 lakh grant, but by October 2026 no application to bring a body home from Russia had reached it, because with no proof of death nobody can apply, and so nobody is getting any compensation (see If a worker dies abroad, what does the family get?) <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.
