<a id="where-to-get-help-8"></a>

### 13.10 Where to get help

**How men have got home:**

- Solaiman Kabir, forced into the war after falling into a broker's hands, fled the battlefield and took shelter at the Bangladesh Embassy in Moscow, which sent him home; the Foreign Ministry spokesperson said on 9 February 2025 that the embassy would take the necessary steps to bring back anyone else who contacts it and explains their problem <sup>[N279](#n279)</sup>.
- The young man who spoke to Prothom Alo in April 2025 said his commander told him that if the Bangladesh government contacted them about him, it would be possible to take him away <sup>[N284](#n284)</sup>.
- Afzal Hossain Meraj's father applied to the Foreign Ministry on 22 April 2025, alleging his son had been sold to the Russian army; by November 2025 the son had come home, two months earlier, with the help of the Bangladesh Embassy in Moscow <sup>[N281](#n281)</sup>.
- Jahangir Alam of Lakshmipur fell ill after four men in his war camp died, spent two months in hospital, escaped from there and came home through the Bangladesh embassy, seven months after going <sup>[N99](#n99), [N72](#n72)</sup>.
- A wounded survivor got home in August 2026 with help from a doctor and the Bangladesh Embassy in Moscow <sup>[N277](#n277)</sup>.
- Adnan Hossain of Narayanganj, admitted to a Moscow hospital with a splinter in his abdomen, had three operations, escaped after two months in hospital, went to the embassy and came home on 15 September 2026 on a travel pass, a valid travel permit <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. Rajon Hossain was wounded, admitted to hospital and escaped from there to come home <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.

**Where families have gone:** in February 2025 relatives of nine men sent to the battlefield had formed human chains in Dhaka several times, gone more than once to the Russian embassy and to Bangladesh's Foreign Ministry, and written to the Expatriates' Welfare Ministry, and on 16 February 2025 Jafar Sardar's family wrote to the Chief Adviser's office asking for his return and their money; the report of 17 February 2025 records no result <sup>[N293](#n293)</sup>. In July 2026 the families of the group of 30 sent 28 letters to the Expatriates' Welfare Ministry asking for their quick and safe return and for financial help <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. Taslim Sardar organised a human chain in Dhaka with the affected families in October 2026 <sup>[N299](#n299)</sup>.

**Local officials:** in April 2025 the Ashuganj Upazila Nirbahi Officer contacted the Expatriates' Welfare Ministry about Akram Hossain's death and was told the family should come with the necessary papers <sup>[N292](#n292)</sup>; the Madarganj Upazila Nirbahi Officer told the Jamalpur families in June 2026 to contact the embassy if the deaths were true, and said he would ask higher authorities to write to the embassy about the bodies; Madaripur Sadar police said they would investigate if Suruj Kazi's family filed a complaint; Yasin Sheikh's family applied for his body through the local administration; for Abdur Rahim the local MP said in May 2026 that there is a process for bringing a body home and the family had been told to apply under it <sup>[N278](#n278), [N286](#n286), [N287](#n287), [N295](#n295)</sup>. After the Amur explosion the Embassy in Moscow gave two numbers for information on the Bangladeshis affected: +7 916 232 3015 (Counsellor and Head of Chancery) and +7 989 113 9169 (First Secretary, labour welfare wing) <sup>[N305](#n305)</sup>. The SRB asked people to check that the firm is legitimate before paying money or travelling for a job abroad <sup>[N289](#n289)</sup>.

**BRAC's Migration Programme** has received many applications from families with almost the same account since late 2024; it submits applications to the Foreign Ministry for the men's rescue, gives case documents to the police's Criminal Investigation Department (CID), and gives families legal and psychological support <sup>[N281](#n281)</sup>. It has asked the government to trace the Bangladeshis still in Russia <sup>[N277](#n277)</sup>, and in October 2026 its head, Shariful Hasan, called for a hotline and a list of the missing <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Do the two numbers the Embassy in Moscow gave after the Amur explosion also take calls from families of men sent to the front, and what email does the embassy give them?

<a id="student-visas-used-as-work-routes"></a>

## 14 Student visas used as work routes

<a id="how-it-is-sold-11"></a>

### 14.1 How it is sold

Agents tell students a visa lets them work freely, and charge families Tk 6 to 8 lakh or more, steering them to barely functioning colleges (Malaysia) <sup>[N307](#n307)</sup>. In Jordan, brokers charged students large sums for often fake admissions <sup>[N308](#n308)</sup>. A returnee from the United Kingdom told a roundtable in Sylhet in April 2026 that brokers sent him there to study saying he could work legally alongside his studies <sup>[N309](#n309)</sup>. For Australia, some intermediary agencies in Dhaka and other cities ran flashy campaigns saying "you can go on a travel visa and become a student" or "once you get in, you can enrol in some private college, renew the visa and stay", Kawsar Khan, a registered Australian migration consultant, wrote in September 2026 <sup>[N310](#n310)</sup>. He wrote that many Bangladeshi families sell land or break savings to send a child to Australia in the hope that the husband or wife will work there to pay the study costs and that they will somehow become permanent <sup>[N310](#n310)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-10"></a>

### 14.2 What happens

- **Malaysia:** students have been moved after arrival from a university into a Bangladeshi-owned English language centre, whose students can't work <sup>[N311](#n311)</sup>. A Student Pass allows only part-time work in semester breaks, up to 20 hours a week, in listed jobs <sup>[37](#o37)</sup>.
- **Kyrgyzstan:** working on a study visa is working without a permit, and leads to expulsion <sup>[N271](#n271), [N272](#n272)</sup>.
- **Jordan:** since 1 April 2025 any foreign student found working is deported, and the employer is fined at least JD 800 (Jordanian dinars) per student <sup>[N312](#n312)</sup>.
- **Italy:** a student visa used only to get in is revoked if the student has not enrolled by 31 January <sup>[38](#o38)</sup>.
- **Russia:** a medical-school offer came with four months of war service attached (see [Russia: recruited into the war](#russia-recruited-into-the-war)) <sup>[N277](#n277)</sup>.
- **United Kingdom:** the same returnee said he found no legal work, had to work illegally, and was paid GBP 1 an hour where others got GBP 8 <sup>[N309](#n309)</sup>.
- **Australia:** under reforms announced by minister Tony Burke, as Kawsar Khan described them in September 2026 <sup>[N310](#n310)</sup>:
  - a mandatory "No Further Stay" condition is being attached to tourist and visitor visas, so coming on a visit visa and applying inside Australia for a student or other visa is in effect over <sup>[N310](#n310)</sup>;
  - "visa hopping" is being stopped: a student can move only to a higher qualification (for example bachelor's to master's), not to a lower-level vocational or diploma course to keep a visa alive <sup>[N310](#n310)</sup>;
  - newly arriving students, and graduate visa holders, can no longer bring a husband, wife or children, except doctoral researchers; families already in Australia will not be separated <sup>[N310](#n310)</sup>;
  - about 77,000 people who stayed on after their visas expired are being identified and made to leave quickly, with 100 new border force officers and use of Melbourne's old quarantine centre <sup>[N310](#n310)</sup>;
  - Kawsar Khan advised dropping the idea, sold by agents in Dhaka, of "reaching Australia any way first and seeing later", because the days of passing years on visa changes or drawn-out appeals are over, and said those whose main aim is simply to work or to live with family would do better not to come under the current rules <sup>[N310](#n310)</sup>.
- **Cyprus:** Shahriar Ahmed Emon, 22, enrolled a few months earlier at a private university in Larnaca <sup>[N313](#n313)</sup>. On the afternoon of 11 June 2026 he told his mother he had a night-duty job starting that night; on reaching the workplace at 9 pm local time he sent his roommate his location on WhatsApp, and was missing from then on, according to his family <sup>[N314](#n314)</sup>. According to police sources, a man he had met on a bus five days earlier, angered by a remark of his, admitted luring him with the job to a deserted area of Kofinou and stabbing him to death that evening <sup>[N313](#n313)</sup>. Unknown people then demanded ransom from his father in Greece, with social-media posts, which police were still verifying, demanding EUR 10,000 one day and EUR 25,000 the next; according to police sources, the accused admitted staging a fake kidnapping to get money from the family <sup>[N313](#n313)</sup>. The family reported the ransom demand and the location pin to the police, who arrested the suspect on 21 June 2026; he led Larnaca CID to the body <sup>[N313](#n313)</sup>.

<a id="forged-documents-and-impersonation"></a>

## 15 Forged documents and impersonation

<a id="how-it-is-sold-12"></a>

### 15.1 How it is sold

- **Fake visas:** in February 2024 three men arrested at Dhaka airport had each paid a broker Tk 16 to 18 lakh to fly to Qatar on fake visas and then go on illegally to France, Italy and Greece, a DB (Dhaka detective police) deputy commissioner said <sup>[N315 (2024)](#n315)</sup>. In May 2026 two men agreed Tk 30 lakh each for Italy, paid Tk 20 lakh up front, and were given fake Schengen visas <sup>[N29](#n29), [N9](#n9)</sup>.
- **Swapped pages:** a "page substitution" ring copies a genuine Italian visa page into a look-alike's passport <sup>[N316](#n316)</sup>. In July 2026 the CID arrested members of a ring accused of sending a woman to Italy on another woman's visa, and seized 56 passports and 153 seals <sup>[N317](#n317)</sup>.
- **Fake certificates:** a ring sold fake police clearance certificates with counterfeit QR codes linking to a fake website <sup>[N318](#n318)</sup>. In one study, 22% of workers said agents forged their medical certificates and 16% their pre-departure orientation certificates <sup>[N319](#n319)</sup>.
- **A forged police clearance, Chattogram, 2026:** Zakir Hossain of Banshkhali, unemployed and with a criminal case against him, did not apply to the police because of the case, and went to an acquaintance at a local travel agency, according to his brother <sup>[N320](#n320)</sup>. The acquaintance, Abdur Rahman, quoted Tk 1,23,000, the brother said; the family paid Tk 80,000 on 21 July 2026 and the rest on receiving the certificate on 12 August <sup>[N320](#n320)</sup>. Police said making the certificate and the fake website cost the ring Tk 3,000, leaving Tk 1,20,000 profit <sup>[N320](#n320)</sup>. The certificates carried police officers' signatures and seals and a QR code that showed them on a clone of the Bangladesh Police website, so, police said, they could not be told from real ones by looking <sup>[N320](#n320)</sup>. The ring had layers: brokers, and people skilled at making QR codes and websites, Chattogram's Additional Superintendent of Police (Special Branch) said <sup>[N320](#n320)</sup>.
- **Changed ages:** girls have been sent abroad with ages raised through altered birth certificates <sup>[N321 (2020)](#n321)</sup>. In May 2025 a dalal sent a Prothom Alo reporter posing as a customer to a computer shop in front of the Gazipur passport office, where a man quoted Tk 32,000, half in advance, to raise the age by one year on both the voter ID card and the passport, delivered within a month or within 15 days for Tk 2,000 more; correcting an NID properly takes several months, the report says <sup>[N322](#n322)</sup>. The Department of Immigration and Passports' additional director general said in August 2026 that because e-passports take all ten fingerprints, the same person can no longer get a passport under a different identity (see "The passport office" in the Departure bottlenecks doc) <sup>[N45](#n45)</sup>.
- **Rohingya on Bangladeshi passports:** police Special Branch wrote to the Registrar General of births and deaths on 26 May 2024 that 102 people had got birth registrations from various places and made Bangladeshi passports whose permanent addresses proved false; it said they were Rohingya, Myanmar citizens, who got the registrations by fraud through dalal rings, dishonest officials and staff and local representatives <sup>[N323 (2024)](#n323)</sup>. In August 2025 two Rohingya from Balukhali camp told Feni police that the dalal Md Abdul Mannan (34) of Parshuram and his associates brought them from the camp with the promise of Bangladeshi passports and sending abroad and kept them at Parshuram on the Indian border to be trafficked to India; police seized 3 Bangladeshi passports, 17 NIDs (national identity cards) and several birth certificates from him and filed a case under the anti-trafficking and passport forgery laws <sup>[N324](#n324)</sup>.
- **Fake players and officials:** some sports federations and associations have for years been alleged to take fake players and officials abroad with their teams as a way of trafficking people, mostly in the smaller sports, Prothom Alo reported in October 2025 <sup>[N325](#n325)</sup>. Until October 2025, federations sent the NSC (National Sports Council) only the players' names, and no one checked whether each was really a player; the chair of the Bangladesh Olympic Association's athletes commission said she often hears that some federations take fake players along <sup>[N325](#n325)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-11"></a>

### 15.2 What happens

- **At Dhaka airport:** in the February 2024 case DB said low-ranking employees of agencies working at the airport and people from private airlines had formed a ring that sent people to the Middle East, Europe and elsewhere on fake visas for lakhs of taka; one of the two alleged ringleaders arrested was a booking assistant of "Qatar Airlines", as the report names it, and the other a security worker at the airport <sup>[N315 (2024)](#n315)</sup>. Police seized three passports, three fake visas, a special card for entering the airport, phones, e-tickets, and work permit and visa papers <sup>[N315 (2024)](#n315)</sup>.
- **At the airport abroad:** the two men with fake Schengen visas were detained at Rome airport for two days and deported <sup>[N29](#n29)</sup>. In June 2026 a Biman ground officer was arrested, accused of helping them board <sup>[N9](#n9)</sup>.
- **At the travel agency:** the forged Chattogram police clearance was detected when Zakir Hossain handed it to a travel agency; he complained in writing to the Chattogram District Superintendent of Police's office, and a case was filed at Banshkhali police station with his brother as plaintiff <sup>[N320](#n320)</sup>.
- **At the embassy:** Italy suspended all Bangladeshi work nulla osta in October 2024 because of the number of false documents, and refuses or revokes a nulla osta obtained by fraud <sup>[39 (2024)](#o39), [40](#o40)</sup>. The Italian Embassy says forged documents mean immediate refusal, and the visa centre's rules say applicants caught with fake papers are banned from applying and reported to the police <sup>[N326](#n326), [41 (2019)](#o41)</sup>. In February 2024 the US Embassy in Dhaka filed a case against five Bangladeshis after two of them, Mofizur Rahman and Ashraful Alam Bhuiyan, admitted at their US visa interview on 11 February that they trafficked people from Bangladesh to various countries; they had submitted fake documents describing themselves as garment businessmen <sup>[N73 (2024)](#n73), [N28 (2024)](#n28)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-10"></a>

### 15.3 The law

- **Bangladesh:** making, obtaining, supplying or holding a document to help migrant smuggling carries 1 to 3 years' rigorous imprisonment and a fine of at least Tk 50,000 under the 2026 Act <sup>[3](#o3)</sup>. A trafficking victim cannot be charged for getting or holding a false travel document as a direct result of the trafficking <sup>[3](#o3)</sup>.
- **Destinations:** Italy punishes forging or using a forged visa with 1 to 6 years in prison; Malaysia treats a holder of forged travel documents as a prohibited immigrant; Singapore jailed a worker in 2021 who bought a forged degree through an agent <sup>[22](#o22), [32 (2006)](#o32), [N327 (2021)](#n327)</sup>.
- **Travellers are charged too:** in the February 2024 Dhaka airport case, the three men trying to travel on fake visas were arrested with the two alleged ringleaders, a case of fraud and forgery was filed against them, and a court granted one day's remand <sup>[N315 (2024)](#n315)</sup>.
- **The Chattogram police clearance ring:** police arrested four members, Md. Ashraf Ali Khan, Md. Nishan, Mohammad Abdullah Al Mamun and Md. Abdur Rahman, on 16 August 2026 in Chattogram city and Banshkhali; police said the four were mainly brokers and were looking for the others <sup>[N320](#n320)</sup>.
- **Sports teams:** since 9 October 2025 the NSC requires a federation's proposal for the government order (GO) letting a team go abroad to reach it at least 10 days before the flight, with proof of the players' fitness and performance; an NSC source said the rule is mainly to stop trafficking, while its director (sports) said only that the question of trafficking in the name of sport does come up <sup>[N325](#n325)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-get-help-9"></a>

### 15.4 Where to get help

CID asks the public to report forged visas and migrant smuggling directly to it (see [Where victims and families get help](#where-victims-and-families-get-help)) <sup>[N29](#n29)</sup>.

<a id="asylum-claims-built-on-a-false-story"></a>

## 16 Asylum claims built on a false story

<a id="how-it-is-sold-13"></a>

### 16.1 How it is sold

- **The package.** A BBC investigation published in April 2026 reported that lawyers and advisers in the UK were helping some migrants claim asylum as gay, and secretly recorded a legal adviser, Tanisa Khan, saying she could arrange a "full package" including photos taken at gay clubs and other evidence <sup>[N328](#n328)</sup>.
- **The second claim.** The UK Home Office said many Bangladeshi men first claimed asylum on political grounds and, after refusal, claimed again as gay; a ring is believed to have helped prepare dozens of claims by Bangladeshi men saying they were gay and that their lives would be in danger if returned <sup>[N328](#n328)</sup>.
- **The price and contents.** According to the head of the Home Office's regional criminal investigations, the suspects charged each asylum seeker GBP 1,000 to 4,500 for fake papers, which could include support letters from Apnajon, a Bangladeshi LGBT organisation in Whitechapel, east London, staged photos meant to show the applicant as gay, and letters from someone claiming to be in a same-sex relationship with the applicant <sup>[N328](#n328)</sup>.
- **Canada.** For people who reached Canada on visit visas built on one set of documents and then claimed asylum on a different story, see Visit and tourist visas sold as jobs <sup>[N160 (2024)](#n160)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-12"></a>

### 16.2 What happens

- **Detection.** Apnajon came under suspicion after caseworkers saw the same kind of information repeated across several suspicious claims, followed by months of investigation <sup>[N328](#n328)</sup>.
- **Arrests.** On 6 October 2026 Home Office immigration officers raided several London addresses and arrested two people on suspicion of fraud and immigration offences, accused of making fake documents, including staged photos and forged papers, for up to GBP 4,500 each; phones, jewellery and thousands of pounds were seized, and a third person was arrested on suspicion of staying illegally <sup>[N328](#n328)</sup>.
- **Claims under review.** The Home Office was reviewing more than a hundred asylum claims linked to the ring in October 2026, and Chris Foster, its regional head of criminal investigations, said some fake claims had undoubtedly already been approved <sup>[N328](#n328)</sup>.

<a id="the-numbers-9"></a>

### 16.3 The numbers

According to a Reuters report of 1 March 2024 cited by Ali Riaz in Prothom Alo, 40,332 Bangladeshis sought asylum in Europe in 2023, against 33,731 the year before <sup>[N329 (2024)](#n329)</sup>. Worldwide, by UNHCR statistics reported in June 2025, 108,131 Bangladeshis sought asylum in 2024, against 75,867 in 2023, 61,298 in 2022, 65,495 in 2021 and 64,636 in 2020, and 28,473 were registered with the UN as refugees in 2024; most registered in Europe and the Americas <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>.

**Why people claim:** government officials told Prothom Alo in June 2025 that a claim needs a risk to life at home and that most Bangladeshi applicants in 2020 to 2024 took advantage of the political situation at home; diplomats said most left for economic reasons, with fewer earning chances during Covid, narrowing legal routes abroad and the political conflict of 2023 and 2024 adding to the trend <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-11"></a>

### 16.4 The law

The UK Home Secretary said in October 2026 that fraudulent asylum claims would be cancelled and the claimants removed from the country, and that those who profited would be prosecuted and their earnings seized; the UK government says conviction for asylum fraud can carry up to 10 years in prison <sup>[N328](#n328)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-in-bangladesh"></a>

## 17 The law in Bangladesh

<a id="the-2026-trafficking-and-smuggling-act"></a>

### 17.1 The 2026 trafficking and smuggling Act

The Human Trafficking and Migrant Smuggling Prevention and Suppression Act, 2026 (Act No. 55 of 2026), passed in April 2026, repealed the Prevention and Suppression of Human Trafficking Act 2012 and took effect at once <sup>[N249](#n249), [3](#o3)</sup>. It replaced an ordinance of the same name, first approved by the interim government's Council of Advisers on 27 November 2025 <sup>[N330](#n330), [3](#o3)</sup>. For the first time it separates trafficking and smuggling as two crimes <sup>[N331](#n331)</sup>. It applies to anyone who commits one of its offences against a Bangladeshi abroad <sup>[3](#o3)</sup>. A Home Ministry joint secretary said in July 2026 that it also covers fraud through fake online job adverts, and that the biggest challenge now is enforcing it and preventing fraud inside the regular migration process <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>.

- **Trafficking:** at least 5 years' rigorous imprisonment, up to life, and a fine of at least Tk 50,000; by an organised gang, death or life imprisonment, or at least 7 years, and a fine of at least Tk 5 lakh <sup>[3](#o3)</sup>.
- **What counts as exploitation:** among other things, forced labour, debt bondage, forcing someone into crimes such as online scamming, and holding someone to demand ransom <sup>[3](#o3)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported in October 2026 that the Act also counts adoption "with dishonest intent" as exploitation, and that selling, buying or handing over a child for unlawful and immoral purposes is punished under sections 372 and 373 of the Penal Code 1860; in Facebook adoption groups it reviewed, one woman offered to pay a pregnant woman's caesarean costs and take the child to Italy, and another sought a baby girl to take to Australia <sup>[N332](#n332)</sup>.
- **Migrant smuggling** (getting someone into another country illegally for gain): 3 to 10 years' rigorous imprisonment and a fine of at least Tk 1 lakh; if the migrant is gravely hurt, dies, is put in danger or treated cruelly, 5 years to life <sup>[3](#o3)</sup>.
- **Documents and ads:** making, obtaining, supplying or holding a document to help smuggling, 1 to 3 years; advertising for smuggling online, on social media or in print, 3 to 7 years and a fine of at least Tk 1 lakh <sup>[3](#o3)</sup>.
- **Victims:** a trafficking victim cannot be charged for illegal entry or stay abroad, or for getting or holding a false travel or identity document, if it was a direct result of the trafficking <sup>[3](#o3)</sup>. Threatening a victim, the family or a witness carries 3 to 7 years, and pressuring a victim or family to settle 2 to 5 years <sup>[3](#o3)</sup>.
- **Filing a case:** anyone can complain at a police station or to the tribunal; the police must protect the complainant and keep their identity confidential <sup>[3](#o3)</sup>. All offences are cognisable, non-bailable and can't be settled privately <sup>[3](#o3)</sup>.
- **Powers:** on the investigator's application the tribunal can freeze bank accounts, block the sale of property, and bar an accused from leaving the country for up to 60 days; an accused who absconds can be tried in absence <sup>[3](#o3)</sup>.
- **Courts:** the government can set up a Human Trafficking and Migrant Smuggling Suppression Tribunal in each district, and it can try a connected offence in the same case <sup>[3](#o3)</sup>. In 2026 Justice and Care, an NGO, was supporting about 400 cases in 18 tribunals <sup>[N261](#n261)</sup>.

<a id="the-overseas-employment-and-migrants-act-2013"></a>

### 17.2 The Overseas Employment and Migrants Act 2013

- **False job promises (s.31):** taking money on a false promise of a job abroad, or keeping a migrant's passport or papers, is punished by up to 5 years in prison and a fine of at least Tk 1 lakh <sup>[2](#o2)</sup>.
- **Visa trading (s.33, amended 2023):** getting a demand letter, visa or work permit illegally, or buying or selling one in Bangladesh, is punished by 2 to 7 years' rigorous imprisonment <sup>[2](#o2)</sup>.
- **Unregistered dalals (s.35, added 2023):** acting as an agency's sub-agent without registration is punished by 1 year in prison or a fine of Tk 50,000 to 2 lakh <sup>[42 (2023)](#o42)</sup>. Agencies may appoint only registered sub-agents <sup>[42 (2023)](#o42)</sup>.
- **Leaving only by air:** BMET's website, as cited by Prothom Alo in July 2025, says a worker must leave with BMET's emigration clearance (smart card) by lawful means, and that going by sea or by any route other than the three international airports, Dhaka, Chattogram and Sylhet, is a punishable offence <sup>[N333](#n333)</sup>. Speakers at a BRAC and Prothom Alo roundtable in Sylhet in April 2026 said there is no lawful way to go abroad by sea, and that no one can lawfully go abroad for work except through the Dhaka, Chattogram and Sylhet airports <sup>[N309](#n309)</sup>.
- **No bail:** offences under ss.31, 33 and 34 are non-bailable and can't be settled privately <sup>[2](#o2)</sup>.
- **Complaint without a court case (s.41):** anyone cheated can complain to the government against any person, including an agency <sup>[2](#o2)</sup>.
- **Legal aid (s.27):** migrants and people defrauded in the name of migration have a right to legal aid <sup>[2](#o2)</sup>.
- **Mobile courts** under the Act give immediate remedies to would-be and returned migrants, free, in 7 working days <sup>[5](#o5)</sup>.
- **Licences cancelled or withdrawn:** according to Ministry sources in July 2026, an agency's licence is cancelled when it breaks the licence conditions and withdrawn when it commits a serious offence or is a threat to national security; getting a withdrawn licence back may take a legal fight <sup>[N334](#n334)</sup>.
- **Women:** Farida Yasmin, a Supreme Court lawyer advising the Bangladesh Migrant Women Workers Association, said in January 2024 that the amended Act still does not separately address the protection of women <sup>[N335 (2024)](#n335)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-to-trafficking-cases"></a>

### 17.3 What happens to trafficking cases

- **2018 to 2025 (CID):** the Trafficking in Human Being (THB) unit of the CID (the police's Criminal Investigation Department) counts 1,657 people charged in 536 cases, of whom only 353 could be arrested <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>.
- **2020 to 2024 (Home Ministry):** in about 4,400 to 4,500 cases under the 2012 Act, 94 to 95% of the accused were acquitted; a Prothom Alo editorial added that in most incidents no case is filed at all <sup>[N72](#n72), [N90](#n90)</sup>.
- **2021 and 2022 (Home Ministry sources):** no accused was convicted in a single trafficking case <sup>[N234 (2024)](#n234)</sup>.
- **January to September 2023 (Home Ministry):** courts disposed of 332 cases, and in 316 of them (95.18%) all the accused were acquitted; 12,279 accused were acquitted, and in the 16 cases that ended in punishment 56 people were sentenced, two of them to life <sup>[N234 (2024)](#n234), [N73 (2024)](#n73)</sup>. In the same nine months 683 new cases were filed, with charge sheets in 31 and final reports in 18; of 30,539 cases in courts up to September 2023, 25,000 were under trial and 5,327 more under investigation <sup>[N234 (2024)](#n234)</sup>. Cases under the Act had been filed against 289,472 people, of whom 132,638 had been arrested <sup>[N234 (2024)](#n234)</sup>.
- **2023 (US State Department's 2024 trafficking report):** the government identified 1,210 trafficking victims (210 of sex trafficking, 795 of forced labour, 205 other), while civil society and international organisations counted at least 10,135, 1,784 of them of sex trafficking; the report says the government does not regularly publish its anti-trafficking data <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>. The same report, published on 24 June 2024, kept Bangladesh on Tier 2 (not fully meeting the minimum standards but making significant efforts); it said the government had formally adopted guidelines for identifying victims, identified more victims than before and amended its migrant worker policy to watch recruiting agents more closely, but had not adequately investigated and prosecuted cases of exploited Rohingya refugees and had set up no new anti-trafficking tribunal <sup>[N336 (2024)](#n336)</sup>.
- **2024 (US State Department's 2025 trafficking report):** Bangladesh stayed on Tier 2; the government identified 1,462 trafficking victims (144 of sex trafficking, 285 of forced labour and 1,033 of other kinds), against 1,210 in the previous report, and the Home Ministry allocated Tk 621.49 million to continue the National Plan of Action 2018-25 on prevention and awareness <sup>[N337](#n337)</sup>.
- **2025 (US State Department's 2026 trafficking report):** the report, published on 8 October 2026, kept Bangladesh on Tier 2: overall efforts had increased on the previous year, and the government was making significant efforts but did not fully meet the minimum standards <sup>[N338](#n338)</sup>. It credited the 2026 trafficking and smuggling Ordinance, more investigations and prosecutions, updated guidelines for identifying trafficking crimes and victims, and a pilot digital system to speed help to victims and coordinate agencies <sup>[N338](#n338)</sup>. It said fewer traffickers were convicted and fewer victims identified than before, that the government did too little against trafficking inside the country, that protection and rehabilitation were inadequate, especially for Rohingya refugees and returned migrant workers, and that the government had not ended the practice of workers paying recruitment fees themselves, which leaves many in debt and at greater risk of trafficking <sup>[N338](#n338)</sup>.
- **2024 (Home Ministry):** 1,135 cases were filed at police stations and 4,291 were pending, 1,310 of them under investigation; one third of all the accused had been arrested; trials of earlier cases ended with 5 people sentenced to life, 55 to other terms and 1,250 acquitted <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
- **Mymensingh division, to January 2025:** a divisional workshop on 28 January 2025 heard that 163 trafficking cases had been filed in the division (52 in Mymensingh district, 12 in Jamalpur, 89 in Netrokona and 10 in Sherpur, up to 2024) and only two disposed of, because the division had no tribunal <sup>[N236](#n236)</sup>.
- **Cox's Bazar, 2012 to August 2025:** 462 trafficking cases were filed in the district and were pending in three separate tribunals, BNWLA's case management coordinator said in September 2025; speakers at the same meeting said delays let offenders stay out of reach and make victims' families reluctant to file cases <sup>[N202](#n202)</sup>.
- **2025 to May 2026 (Home Ministry):** 1,126 new cases were filed in 2025, and up to May 2026 5,284 cases were pending, 3,306 under trial and 1,978 under investigation <sup>[N72](#n72)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported that in 2026, 71 new cases were filed in January, 66 in February, 110 in March, 130 in April and 123 in May, while only 17 were disposed of in those five months <sup>[N72](#n72)</sup>.

<a id="why-cases-fail"></a>

### 17.4 Why cases fail

- **Investigation:** experts told Prothom Alo in January 2024 that accused are acquitted because cases are not investigated properly, witnesses can't be produced in court, and cases are settled with the trafficking rings for money <sup>[N234 (2024)](#n234)</sup>. Salma Ali, president of BNWLA (the Bangladesh National Women Lawyers' Association), said then that trafficking cases are very profitable for the police: investigating officers take money from both the victim and the accused and hold up the investigation for a long time, and ring members, who are very influential, threaten victims into a compromise <sup>[N234 (2024)](#n234), [N73 (2024)](#n73)</sup>.
- **No dedicated investigators:** the legal coordinator of RMMRU (the Refugee and Migratory Movements Research Unit) said in July 2026 that the trafficking tribunals have no designated investigation agency of their own, so investigation reports are late and often no proper investigation is done, and that the Dhaka tribunal had had no judge for five months <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>.
- **Trafficking inside legal migration:** C R Abrar of RMMRU said in July 2024 that trafficking can also happen within the labour migration process but the government does not acknowledge it, and recruiting agencies keep themselves clear <sup>[N20 (2024)](#n20)</sup>. He also said such cases are hard to prove as trafficking and are often directed to be tried under the 2013 migration Act instead of the anti-trafficking law <sup>[N339 (2024)](#n339)</sup>.
- **Small fry caught:** a Prothom Alo editorial of 19 August 2026 said that after each big disaster one or two local dalals are caught while the ringleaders stay out of reach, and those arrested are hardly ever tried because of weak FIRs (first information reports) and lack of evidence <sup>[N50](#n50)</sup>.
- **Police refusing the case:** in the Faridpur case over a woman trafficked to India in 2010, Madhukhali police would not record it under the trafficking law and the father filed it directly at the tribunal (see [India: women and girls taken across the land border](#india-women-and-girls-taken-across-the-land-border)) <sup>[N240](#n240)</sup>.
- **Fines instead of prison:** the US State Department's 2024 trafficking report said courts fine most traffickers instead of jailing them, and that because recruitment fees are still allowed many migrant workers fall into debt, which raises their risk of being trafficked <sup>[N336 (2024)](#n336)</sup>.
- **Victims who don't see it as trafficking:** Shariful Hasan of BRAC's Migration Programme said in July 2024 that a recent BRAC study found only 5% of ordinary people understand migration and trafficking as separate things, the rest not realising they are being trafficked, and that people keep everything secret before going, on the broker's advice <sup>[N339 (2024)](#n339)</sup>.
- **Victims who never file:** A K M Masud Ali of INCIDIN Bangladesh said in January 2025 that the real number of trafficking cases is far higher than published figures, because many victims do not dare to file cases for social and legal reasons <sup>[N236](#n236)</sup>. Salma Ali of BNWLA said in July 2024 that there is no protection for victims and witnesses and that the main masterminds stay out of reach <sup>[N20 (2024)](#n20)</sup>. In the Rohingya camps, IOM said in September 2025, more than 600 incidents identified in one year did not become cases because of long delays in court, compromise settlements, intimidation by offenders, lack of witnesses and lack of money <sup>[N202](#n202)</sup>.
- **Time and money:** he said a case takes two to three years to go from investigation to trial, many victims can't keep it going because they spent lakhs to go abroad and have nothing left, and many lose a day's income every time they go to court <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>. Salma Ali said in July 2026 that cases drag on for lack of skilled public prosecutors and judges <sup>[N244](#n244)</sup>.
- **Confidentiality:** she also said many victims stop pursuing a case in the end over the question of confidentiality, so organised criminals get away, and that trafficking in the name of migration continues through land ports, airports and sea routes <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>.
- **Known traffickers left alone:** the chairperson of OKUP (Ovibashi Karmi Unnayan Program, a migrant workers' organisation) said in July 2026 that at a recent meeting in Madaripur the deputy commissioner (the district's top administrator) said he knows the traffickers but has no power to act against them <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>.
- **What investigators are told to establish:** after no convictions in 2021 and 2022 the Home Ministry instructed investigators that trafficking happens in six stages from going abroad to coming back, and to establish who trafficked the victim, who introduced the victim to the ring, whether the victim was threatened, taken by force or trafficked by exploiting a vulnerability, and the route the ring used <sup>[N234 (2024)](#n234)</sup>.

<a id="cases-3"></a>

### 17.5 Cases

- 2019 to 2025: OKUP supported the case of an abused woman who came back from the Middle East in 2019; a long investigation found elements of trafficking, in 2025 the special tribunal's judge proposed an out-of-court settlement, and though she said she wanted justice and dignity, not money, the case was settled for a little over Tk 4 lakh, according to OKUP's chairperson <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>.
- October 2024 to April 2025: Imran Ahmad, Minister of Expatriates' Welfare and Overseas Employment from 2019 (state minister from 2018) and again after the 2024 election, was arrested by Dhaka Metropolitan Detective Police in Banani on the night of 20 October 2024 and remanded the next day in a case filed at Paltan police station under the trafficking Act; on 30 April 2025 the ACC (the Anti-Corruption Commission) filed a further case against him over BDT 90 lakh of illegal assets and BDT 6 crore of abnormal transactions <sup>[N340](#n340)</sup>.
- January 2024, Savar: the father of a woman trafficked to India on a promise of Canada said that after he filed a trafficking case the accused filed a false case against him at Savar police station, he was arrested and spent four months in jail at a cost of Tk 5 to 6 lakh, and he had heard all the accused were acquitted <sup>[N234 (2024)](#n234), [N73 (2024)](#n73)</sup>. He said the investigating officer told him many times he would be taken to court to testify but never took him, and that two of the acquitted were back in the area; the sub-inspector who investigated said the case had long since gone to the CID and he had no update <sup>[N234 (2024)](#n234)</sup>.
- July 2026: Mannan Soyal, back from a scam centre in Cambodia, told a Prothom Alo roundtable in Dhaka that after he filed a trafficking case against the people who cheated him, the accused filed a counter-case, so he is now himself an accused and has no money to fight it, and he alleged the fraudsters were pressing him to take money and withdraw <sup>[N244](#n244), [N159](#n159)</sup>.

<a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help"></a>

## 18 Where victims and families get help

1. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-1"></a>**In danger in Bangladesh:** call 999, the police's national emergency line, free, 24 hours <sup>[43](#o43)</sup>.
2. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-2"></a>**File a criminal case with the local police.** CID asks returnees to file cases with their local police so the brokers and agencies can be prosecuted <sup>[N2](#n2)</sup>. Under the 2026 Act a case can be filed at a police station or directly with the tribunal, and the police must protect the complainant and keep their identity confidential <sup>[3](#o3)</sup>. A Chandpur family's case in December 2025 led to an arrest <sup>[N32](#n32)</sup>. Two youths trafficked to Cambodia came home on 3 September 2026, a case naming two men was filed at Paltan police station, Dhaka, on 26 October, and the CID arrested both the next day, according to the CID <sup>[N245](#n245)</sup>. In 2020 to 2024, 94 to 95% of the accused in trafficking cases were acquitted (see [What happens to trafficking cases](#what-happens-to-trafficking-cases)) <sup>[N72](#n72)</sup>.
3. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-3"></a>**Report to the CID.** The CID's Trafficking in Human Being (THB) Unit leads trafficking investigations nationwide and asks the public to report trafficking, forged visas and smuggling to it <sup>[44](#o44), [N29](#n29)</sup>. A Home Ministry joint secretary said in January 2025 that from 12 February 2025 victims could also file a trafficking complaint online, and that police, BGB and the other units concerned would get it automatically and help the victim <sup>[N236](#n236)</sup>.
4. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-4"></a>**Airport police.** A deputy inspector general of the Airport Armed Police Battalion (APBn) said in August 2026 that it counsels trafficking victims and returnees, tries to get them rehabilitated through BRAC and other organisations, takes and investigates their cases, has brought victims back from Iran, Libya and Greece, and tries to arrest accused people in Bangladesh even when the crime happened abroad <sup>[N45](#n45)</sup>.
5. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-5"></a>**Call the Probash-Bondhu call centre** of the Wage Earners' Welfare Board, 24 hours: 16135 free in Bangladesh, +8809610102030 from abroad <sup>[28](#o28)</sup>.
6. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-6"></a>**Apply to the Welfare Board.**
   - **District office:** the Probashi Kallyan Centre is the Board's district office; in Cumilla it began work in July 2023, taking over services the district manpower office used to give, and an application through it for a migrant in trouble abroad gets legal help with the embassy's assistance <sup>[N341](#n341)</sup>.
   - **Membership:** a Board director said in May 2026 that anyone, documented or undocumented, can become a member for Tk 400, and that the Board gives a member who falls ill, is jailed or is in danger abroad legal help through the embassy's labour wing <sup>[N342](#n342)</sup>. He also said that with a Tk 300 membership a dead worker's body is brought home from any country, Tk 35,000 is paid for burial and transport, a further Tk 3 lakh goes to the heirs, and up to Tk 10 lakh more is paid if there is insurance (see If a worker dies abroad, what does the family get?) <sup>[N342](#n342)</sup>.
   - **Money for illness, injury and children:** a worker who applies because of illness within one year of coming home gets a one-time grant of up to Tk 2 lakh (see "What help is there if I'm injured or fall ill abroad?" in the FAQ doc), and a family gets Tk 2,000 a month for up to seven years for each disabled child, for at most two children <sup>[N342](#n342)</sup>. A worker injured or maimed abroad gets up to Tk 4 lakh against a Tk 500 premium, and a five-year life insurance policy costs Tk 1,000 <sup>[N342](#n342)</sup>.
   - **Unpaid money abroad:** the Board helps recover dues owed abroad through 22 law firms in 12 countries, and has given more than 239,000 people training, counselling and financial help under its reintegration policy <sup>[N342](#n342)</sup>. The Cumilla centre's assistant director said the Board helps through the embassy to recover unpaid insurance and compensation, that embassies can appoint lawyers, that safe homes for women workers operate abroad, that there is a dormitory for overnight stays near the airport, and that the Board gives education stipends to migrants' children and an allowance for disabled children <sup>[N341](#n341)</sup>.
   - **An example:** on an application by Anirban Survivor Voice Foundation, the Board wrote to the labour wing of the Bangladesh embassy in Bangkok asking it to arrange the release and quick return of 30 Bangladeshis held in Cambodian immigration detention; they flew home on 20 October 2026 <sup>[N255](#n255)</sup>.
7. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-7"></a>**Complain against an agency to BMET** (Bureau of Manpower, Employment and Training): a written application with proof to the Director General <sup>[4](#o4)</sup>. Director (Emigration): +8802222222415, 01711-135901, diremi@bmet.gov.bd <sup>[4](#o4)</sup>. Hotline of OEP (the Overseas Employment Platform, BMET's online system): 09614445271 <sup>[45](#o45)</sup>.
8. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-8"></a>**Complain to the Ministry of Expatriates' Welfare:** enforcement branch, with proof, your address and the agency's licence (RL) number <sup>[5](#o5)</sup>. From abroad, email the complaint cell at minister.expat@gmail.com <sup>[46](#o46)</sup>.
9. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-9"></a>**If the family is threatened at home,** apply to the Welfare Board directly or through the expatriate welfare branch of the Deputy Commissioner's (district administration) office or the DEMO (District Employment and Manpower Office); the Board writes to the local authorities <sup>[47](#o47)</sup>.
10. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-10"></a>**District and union committees:** Cox's Bazar has a District Vigilance Task Force Committee and a District Migration Coordination Committee against unsafe migration, and counter-trafficking committees exist down to union level, though an NGO head there said they need to be made active (see "Local committees and mediation" in the Dalal scams doc) <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>.
11. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-11"></a>**At the airport on return:** the Welfare Board's desk at Dhaka airport, +88 02 8901040, +880 1971 430203 <sup>[48](#o48)</sup>. Returnees from Cambodia and Europe got emergency help, counselling and money to get home there from BRAC's Migration Programme <sup>[N246](#n246), [N147](#n147)</sup>. BRAC's Migration Programme said in March 2025 that 250 to 300 migrant workers a day were coming back empty-handed, without proper clothes or money, and that it gives each Tk 1,000 for the fare home <sup>[N343](#n343)</sup>.
12. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-12"></a>**BRAC Migration Programme:** airport help, counselling, money or materials to restart earning, and legal help, for returnees and trafficking survivors <sup>[6](#o6)</sup>. It said in April 2026 that it had helped about 39,000 people at the airport in the previous eight years, and gives returnees reintegration support and training, and then counselling, and is trying to set them up as entrepreneurs <sup>[N48](#n48)</sup>. In August 2026 it said it had helped 41,000 migrants at the airport in eight years and handed 164 safely to their families; one, Mohammad Farid, deported from Turkey and able to name only his parents and "Ukhiya, Cox's Bazar", was passed by AVSEC (the Civil Aviation Authority's aviation security force) through the Probashi Kallyan desk to BRAC's Ashkona Migration Welfare Centre, whose staff found his family in Ukhiya within 12 hours with the help of local representatives and journalists, and the director of RAISE, a Wage Earners' Welfare Board project, said he would be given the help he needs <sup>[N132](#n132)</sup>. At Dhaka's Hazrat Shahjalal International Airport, when a mentally ill worker or one with no address is found, the Probashi Kallyan desk or the Airport Armed Police phone Al-Amin (Nayan), manager of BRAC's Migration Welfare Centre, who by the US State Department's account at its June 2024 TIP (Trafficking in Persons) Hero award had helped at least 34,000 returnees and trafficking victims <sup>[N344 (2024)](#n344)</sup>. Al-Amin said he was himself trafficked when he went to work in Malaysia, which Prothom Alo reported was in 2007; he came home the same year, started work in BRAC's migration programme in 2017, and is president of Anirban Survivor Voice, the largest network of trafficking survivors in Bangladesh <sup>[N344 (2024)](#n344)</sup>. Roksana Akter of Bishwanath, Sylhet, abused at her workplace in Saudi Arabia and forced to borrow to come home, said in April 2026 that the EU and BRAC's Prottasha-2 project helped her set up a shop whose income now runs her household <sup>[N48](#n48)</sup>. BRAC Information Center: Rahman Villa (2nd floor), 402 Ashkona, opposite the airport, Dakshinkhan, Dhaka; 01700797182, 01700797183 <sup>[N345](#n345)</sup>.
13. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-13"></a>**Women trafficked to India:** BNWLA (the Bangladesh National Women Lawyers' Association) rehabilitated a woman from near the Jashore border rescued from an Indian brothel, BGB (Border Guard Bangladesh) has handed victims it rescued to Justice and Care, an NGO, and Justice and Care said in early 2025 that the return of at least 10 women and girls trafficked across the border since August 2024 and rescued by Indian police was under way <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
14. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-14"></a>**IOM** (International Organization for Migration, the UN migration agency) runs the return flights from Libya with the Embassy and the Ministries <sup>[N30](#n30), [N16](#n16)</sup>.
15. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-15"></a>**Embassies:** Bangkok for Cambodia and Thailand, and for people rescued from Myawaddy in Myanmar; Tashkent for Kyrgyzstan; Rome for Italy; Kuala Lumpur for Malaysia; Doha, +974 44671927, hotline +974 33662000 <sup>[34](#o34), [N250](#n250), [N275 (2024)](#n275), [16](#o16), [33](#o33), [49](#o49)</sup>.

The US State Department's 2025 trafficking report said the government had formally adopted a National Referral Mechanism, and that identified victims get health care, legal aid and shelter through centres run by the Ministries of Women and Children Affairs, Social Welfare, and Expatriates' Welfare and Overseas Employment <sup>[N337](#n337)</sup>. Its 2024 report said there were few shelters outside Dhaka, especially for male victims, and that the government had for the first time set up a fund to compensate trafficking victims <sup>[N336 (2024)](#n336)</sup>. The Home Ministry said in July 2026 it was working on a national referral system and a National Plan of Action 2026-30 so that victims get protection, legal aid and services in a coordinated way <sup>[N159](#n159)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What phone or email does the CID's THB Unit give the public for trafficking tips?
> - What is the web address of the Home Ministry's online trafficking complaint form announced for 12 February 2025, and do complaints made there reach an investigator?
> - How does a trafficking victim apply to the government's victim compensation fund, and has anyone been paid from it?
> - Is the Welfare Board membership fee Tk 400 or Tk 300, and how does a worker already abroad without clearance pay it?
> - What number does the Airport Armed Police Battalion give victims who want to file a case?
> - What numbers do BNWLA and Justice and Care give families of women trafficked to India?
